Higher Education and Research English

A higher education and research English curriculum for grant proposals, lab meetings, peer review, research ethics, authorship, data management, academic presentations, and institutional collaboration.

Words into action

Make yourself clear.

For faculty, postdoctoral researchers, graduate students, lab managers, research administrators, grant staff, ethics-board coordinators, and academic program leaders.

What you will practice · Choose your pace

What you will practice

  • Specify a research question and distinguish an observed association from a causal effect.
  • Align a grant aim with pilot scope and interpret predefined feasibility milestones.
  • Present an experimental discrepancy, disclose a method change, and propose a controlled check without editing the record to fit a preferred result.
  • Describe a secondary-use proposal and seek an authorized ethics determination without assuming coded data is anonymous.
  • Discuss authorship eligibility, outstanding responsibilities, and author order without granting or denying credit by status.

Choose your pace

Quick practice · 15 minutes: complete one short exchange, check the explanations, and read both roles aloud.

Extended practice · two or three sittings: check the case facts, study the vocabulary and phrases, complete the longer conversation, then rehearse the follow-up exchange.

Level guide: designed for intermediate to advanced learners. B1 learners can start with the short exchanges and supplied phrases; B2 learners can complete the cloze conversations; C1 learners can focus on the distinctions in the language checks. These are teaching suggestions, not a certified level assessment.

Prompts

All cases and figures are fictional. This book teaches research communication, not statistical, clinical, or legal advice or ethics approval. Follow the relevant institution, funder, jurisdiction, and journal requirements; obtain required review before conducting or changing research.

01Research Questions and Study DesignWhat exactly would it mean for tutoring to work?+

Your goal

Specify a research question and distinguish an observed association from a causal effect.

This lesson in the book, page 4 ↗

A Read the situation

What exactly would it mean for tutoring to work?

Doctoral researcher Imani and supervisor Leon revise a proposal asking whether optional tutoring works. Available records cover first-year students in one introductory biology course for one semester: 32 of 40 tutoring participants completed the course, compared with 36 of 60 nonparticipants. Tutoring was self-selected, not randomly assigned. The groups may differ in prior preparation or motivation, which these records do not measure. The immediate analysis can describe the association with completion in this cohort. A future causal study would need a suitable design; it has not been conducted.

Imani: Doctoral researcher / Leon: Research supervisor

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What is the completion rate among tutoring participants?

Answer and explanation

A. 80%

Thirty-two completers divided by forty participants equals eighty percent.

2. What is the difference between the two completion rates?

Answer and explanation

B. Twenty percentage points

Participant completion is eighty percent and nonparticipant completion is sixty percent, a twenty-point difference.

3. Why is a causal claim unsupported here?

Answer and explanation

B. Tutoring was self-selected and relevant group differences are unmeasured.

Self-selection and unmeasured differences prevent the observed contrast from isolating tutoring's causal effect.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

32/40 is 80%; 36/60 is 60%. The difference is 20 percentage points. Relative to 60%, the difference is approximately 33.3%, not 20%. Neither calculation removes the design limitation created by self-selection.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
research question
A specific question a study is designed to answer.refine a research question
target population
The group to which a research question refers.define the target population
study sample
The observed group included in the study.describe the study sample
cohort
A defined group observed over a specified period.follow a student cohort
outcome variable
The measured result of interest.specify the outcome variable
exposure
A condition or experience whose relationship with an outcome is studied.define the exposure
comparison group
The group used as a reference for a contrast.identify the comparison group
observational study
A study observing conditions without assigning the exposure.describe an observational study
self-selection
Entry into a group through participants' own choices.account for self-selection
random assignment
Allocation to study conditions using a random process.distinguish random assignment
random sampling
Selection of units from a population by a random process.distinguish random sampling
association
An observed relationship between variables.report an association
causal effect
A change attributable to an intervention or exposure.estimate a causal effect
confounder
A factor influencing both exposure and outcome that can distort their apparent causal relationship.address potential confounders
baseline difference
A group difference present before the studied exposure or outcome.examine baseline differences
operational definition
A precise specification of how a concept is measured.state an operational definition
completion rate
The proportion meeting the defined completion outcome.calculate the completion rate
denominator
The base count used in a proportion.state the denominator
percentage-point difference
The arithmetic difference between percentages.report a percentage-point difference
relative difference
A difference expressed in relation to a reference value.distinguish a relative difference
internal validity
The credibility of the study's inference within its setting.assess internal validity
external validity
The extent to which findings apply beyond the studied setting.discuss external validity
estimand
The precise quantity a study seeks to estimate.define the estimand
study protocol
A documented plan for the study's conduct and analysis.revise the study protocol

Random assignment concerns allocation to conditions; random sampling concerns selection into the sample. They address different questions. The current case has self-selected tutoring participation, and no broader representativeness or causal design is established.

C Notice the language

Narrowing a claim strengthens the question

A supervisor who challenges the word works may be asking for a testable question, not rejecting the project. Respond with the population, measured outcome, and comparison. Keep the broader ambition visible as a future question instead of presenting it as an answer from the current records.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Clarify the broad verb
"What outcome would count as working?"
Specify the population
"The records cover first-year students in this course."
Set the period
"The observation window is one semester."
Name the outcome
"We are measuring course completion."
Identify exposure
"Tutoring participation was optional."
State the first rate
"Thirty-two of forty participants completed, or 80%."
State the comparison
"Thirty-six of sixty nonparticipants completed, or 60%."
Express the contrast
"That is a 20-percentage-point difference."
Qualify the inference
"This is an observed association, not an isolated causal effect."
Name selection
"Students chose whether to participate."
Identify missing information
"Prior preparation and motivation are not measured here."
Avoid inventing an explanation
"Those are possible group differences, not confirmed causes."
Bound the population claim
"The current result concerns this course cohort."
Separate future work
"A causal question needs a suitable future design."
Preserve the useful result
"The descriptive contrast remains worth reporting."
Close the revision
"The proposal should match its question to the available evidence."
6 language and precision notes
Works
Needs a specified outcome, population, comparison, and time.
Completed
Requires a consistent completion definition for both groups.
Higher
Describes direction without establishing cause.
Associated with
Does not mean caused by.
Random
State whether it concerns sampling or assignment.
May differ
Marks a possible explanation, not a finding.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which headline fits the available evidence?

Answer and explanation

A. Tutoring participation was associated with higher completion in this course cohort.

The supported headline preserves an association; the alternatives add causation, misstate relative change, or confuse sampling with assignment.

2. Which question is still unresolved?

Answer and explanation

C. Whether tutoring caused the completion difference

The current observational records do not isolate the causal contribution of tutoring.

3. Which statement about possible confounding is accurate?

Answer and explanation

D. Prior preparation or motivation may differ, but these records do not establish those differences.

The brief identifies plausible unmeasured factors, not verified explanations or adjusted estimates.

4. Which design distinction is correct?

Answer and explanation

D. Random assignment allocates conditions; random sampling selects sample members.

The two random processes concern different stages and do not substitute for one another.

Check explanations in the book, page 85 ↗

E Conversations

What exactly would it mean for tutoring to work?

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Imani: Doctoral researcher / Leon: Research supervisor

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • research question
  • Random assignment
  • percentage-point difference
  • external validity
  • causal effect
  • outcome variable
  • study protocol
  • denominator
  • self-selection
  • association
  1. Leon
    The proposal asks whether tutoring works. That sounds important, but what outcome would answer the question, and whose experience are we actually observing?
  2. Imani
    I need a sharper 1. The available records concern first-year students in one introductory biology course during one semester. They record tutoring participation and course completion.
  3. Leon
    Then completion, rather than examination performance or long-term retention, is the outcome. We should not let the proposal switch among those meanings without noticing.
  4. Imani
    Agreed. The 2 is completion as recorded for that semester. I will use the same definition for participants and nonparticipants, so the comparison does not change meaning between groups.
  5. Leon
    Walk me through the counts before we discuss the interpretation. Percentages can conceal different group sizes, especially when the audience sees only a headline.
  6. Imani
    The participant 3 is forty, with thirty-two completing. That is eighty percent. Among sixty nonparticipants, thirty-six completed, giving sixty percent for the comparison group.
  7. Leon
    The draft says twenty percent higher. Do you mean twenty percentage points, or are you calculating a relative increase from the nonparticipant rate?
  8. Imani
    Correct. The 4 is twenty. Relative to sixty percent, the difference is about thirty-three point three percent. Neither phrasing, by itself, explains why the groups differ.
  9. Leon
    Were students allocated to tutoring, or did they choose it? That matters more for the causal claim than how confidently we present the percentages.
  10. Imani
    Participation involved 5. Students chose whether to attend. The records do not measure prior preparation or motivation, and either could differ between groups. We have not established those differences.
  11. Leon
    Then the claim that tutoring caused the higher completion rate is stronger than the design supports. We can still report the observed relationship accurately.
  12. Imani
    I will call it an 6 with completion in this cohort. That preserves the finding without treating possible unmeasured group differences as either irrelevant or already proved explanations.
  13. Leon
    A colleague proposed solving the problem by randomly sampling more records from the same optional program. Would that make tutoring itself randomly assigned?
  14. Imani
    No. 7 concerns allocation to conditions. Random sampling concerns which people enter the sample. A larger randomly sampled record set would not retroactively randomize the tutoring decision.
  15. Leon
    Good. We should also avoid implying that one course at one institution establishes what happens for all students in every subject and setting.
  16. Imani
    That is an 8 question. The current description stays with this course and semester. Broader applicability needs evidence across the relevant settings, not just a less cautious title.
  17. Leon
    Let us keep the causal question for the next study. For this paper, can you put the course, semester, and two counts into the abstract?
  18. Imani
    The future 9 question needs a suitable design and explicit assumptions. We should discuss that design as a proposed next step, not imply that it has already been implemented.
  19. Leon
    Revise the objective to describe completion by tutoring participation in this cohort. Then make the design limitation visible in the abstract as well.
  20. Imani
    I will update the 10 and the proposal wording together. The useful result is a bounded comparison with clear counts, while the causal and broader-population questions remain open.

Answers and explanations
  1. research question Turn 2. The research question must specify what is being measured and for whom rather than relying on the broad word works.
  2. outcome variable Turn 4. The outcome variable is course completion, not every possible academic benefit that tutoring might produce.
  3. denominator Turn 6. The denominator is forty tutoring participants; pairing it with thirty-two completers yields the eighty-percent completion rate.
  4. percentage-point difference Turn 8. The percentage-point difference subtracts sixty percent from eighty percent, producing twenty points rather than a twenty-percent relative change.
  5. self-selection Turn 10. Self-selection means students chose tutoring, so the groups may differ in ways other than the tutoring exposure.
  6. association Turn 12. Association describes the observed relationship without claiming that the current design isolates a causal effect.
  7. Random assignment Turn 14. Random assignment allocates study conditions; selecting additional records randomly would not change the original self-selected tutoring exposure.
  8. external validity Turn 16. External validity concerns application beyond the studied course and cohort, which the supplied records do not establish.
  9. causal effect Turn 18. A causal effect is the contribution attributable to tutoring, which requires a suitable design beyond the current descriptive comparison.
  10. study protocol Turn 20. The study protocol should record the precise question, definitions, and analysis boundaries so later reporting follows the same plan.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Leon: The proposal asks whether tutoring works. That sounds important, but what outcome would answer the question, and whose experience are we actually observing?
  2. Imani: I need a sharper research question. The available records concern first-year students in one introductory biology course during one semester. They record tutoring participation and course completion.
  3. Leon: Then completion, rather than examination performance or long-term retention, is the outcome. We should not let the proposal switch among those meanings without noticing.
  4. Imani: Agreed. The outcome variable is completion as recorded for that semester. I will use the same definition for participants and nonparticipants, so the comparison does not change meaning between groups.
  5. Leon: Walk me through the counts before we discuss the interpretation. Percentages can conceal different group sizes, especially when the audience sees only a headline.
  6. Imani: The participant denominator is forty, with thirty-two completing. That is eighty percent. Among sixty nonparticipants, thirty-six completed, giving sixty percent for the comparison group.
  7. Leon: The draft says twenty percent higher. Do you mean twenty percentage points, or are you calculating a relative increase from the nonparticipant rate?
  8. Imani: Correct. The percentage-point difference is twenty. Relative to sixty percent, the difference is about thirty-three point three percent. Neither phrasing, by itself, explains why the groups differ.
  9. Leon: Were students allocated to tutoring, or did they choose it? That matters more for the causal claim than how confidently we present the percentages.
  10. Imani: Participation involved self-selection. Students chose whether to attend. The records do not measure prior preparation or motivation, and either could differ between groups. We have not established those differences.
  11. Leon: Then the claim that tutoring caused the higher completion rate is stronger than the design supports. We can still report the observed relationship accurately.
  12. Imani: I will call it an association with completion in this cohort. That preserves the finding without treating possible unmeasured group differences as either irrelevant or already proved explanations.
  13. Leon: A colleague proposed solving the problem by randomly sampling more records from the same optional program. Would that make tutoring itself randomly assigned?
  14. Imani: No. Random assignment concerns allocation to conditions. Random sampling concerns which people enter the sample. A larger randomly sampled record set would not retroactively randomize the tutoring decision.
  15. Leon: Good. We should also avoid implying that one course at one institution establishes what happens for all students in every subject and setting.
  16. Imani: That is an external validity question. The current description stays with this course and semester. Broader applicability needs evidence across the relevant settings, not just a less cautious title.
  17. Leon: Let us keep the causal question for the next study. For this paper, can you put the course, semester, and two counts into the abstract?
  18. Imani: The future causal effect question needs a suitable design and explicit assumptions. We should discuss that design as a proposed next step, not imply that it has already been implemented.
  19. Leon: Revise the objective to describe completion by tutoring participation in this cohort. Then make the design limitation visible in the abstract as well.
  20. Imani: I will update the study protocol and the proposal wording together. The useful result is a bounded comparison with clear counts, while the causal and broader-population questions remain open.
Dialogue in the book, page 10 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

What does works mean in this proposal?

Fictional study-design meeting using the lesson case: a proposal asks whether a program works but does not identify an outcome or population.

  1. Research supervisor: The research question asks whether the program works. Which outcome and population do you mean?
  2. Graduate researcher: I was thinking about first-year students, but I haven't specified what improvement we would measure.
  3. Research supervisor: Then first-year students is a proposed population, not a complete study definition yet.
  4. Graduate researcher: Could we focus on completion of the introductory course rather than the broad phrase student success?
  5. Research supervisor: That is more specific. We still need to clarify the course, observation period, and comparison.
  6. Graduate researcher: Should I write a directional hypothesis before those choices have been agreed by the team?
  7. Research supervisor: You can propose one, but its methodology and limitations depend on the question we actually define.
  8. Graduate researcher: I'll bring a bounded question about course completion and mark the comparison and time period as unresolved.
  9. Research supervisor: Good. Avoid promising a causal answer before the design supports that interpretation.
  10. Graduate researcher: I'll clarify the question first so the method follows the intended outcome rather than a vague claim.
Compare experiences or estimate an effect?

Fictional research planning: a team proposes interviews about a mentoring program, while a sponsor expects a numerical estimate of its causal effect.

  1. Research coordinator: Our plan proposes interviews, but the sponsor expects a numerical estimate of the mentoring program's effect.
  2. Principal investigator: We need to clarify the research question. Exploring experiences differs from estimating a causal effect.
  3. Research coordinator: Could interviews explain what participants found useful without establishing how much the program changed outcomes?
  4. Principal investigator: Yes. That is a different contribution, with its own methodology and limitations.
  5. Research coordinator: Then we shouldn't present the interview plan as already answering the sponsor's quantitative expectation.
  6. Principal investigator: Correct. Ask which decision the sponsor needs to inform and what evidence would be relevant.
  7. Research coordinator: I'll explain the proposed qualitative focus and identify the effect estimate as outside the current design.
  8. Principal investigator: Please avoid saying one approach is universally better. The question determines which methods are appropriate.
  9. Research coordinator: Can we discuss alternative scope before committing recruitment or a hypothesis the current design cannot test?
  10. Principal investigator: Absolutely. Let's agree the bounded research question and then review the necessary design and approvals.
The population is broader than the available sample

Fictional proposal discussion: a researcher can recruit from one evening seminar but has written a question about all university students.

  1. Research supervisor: Your question concerns all university students, but recruitment is limited to one evening seminar. How do those align?
  2. Graduate researcher: They don't align fully. The available sample is narrower than the population named in the proposal.
  3. Research supervisor: Could you clarify whether the study is exploratory within that seminar or intended to estimate a broader pattern?
  4. Graduate researcher: An exploratory study within the seminar is more realistic with the access currently available.
  5. Research supervisor: Then state that boundary and consider how seminar participation may limit what the findings represent.
  6. Graduate researcher: Should I assume evening students have particular work habits to explain the limitation?
  7. Research supervisor: No. You can identify the recruitment boundary without inventing characteristics that haven't been measured.
  8. Graduate researcher: I'll narrow the research question and keep broader applicability as an open issue, not a promised outcome.
  9. Research supervisor: Good. We can review whether the methodology answers that narrower question before discussing wider claims.
  10. Graduate researcher: I'll bring the revised population, proposed outcome, and remaining design questions to our next meeting.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

What exactly would it mean for tutoring to work?
  1. Read turns 5-8, stressing each denominator and the words percentage points.
  2. Repeat turns 9-14, distinguishing self-selection, random sampling, and random assignment.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

A rate difference is not a randomized effect

In a self-selected workshop cohort, 18 of 30 attendees and 20 of 40 nonattendees completed a task. Prior experience was not measured. The records cover one course only.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Researcher: "The attendee completion rate is ___ percent."

Answer and explanation

A. 60

Eighteen completers divided by thirty attendees equals sixty percent.

2. Supervisor: "The nonattendee rate is ___ percent."

Answer and explanation

A. 50

Twenty completers divided by forty nonattendees equals fifty percent.

3. Researcher: "The difference is ___ percentage points."

Answer and explanation

D. 10

Sixty percent minus fifty percent is ten percentage points.

4. Supervisor: "The contrast establishes an association, not a ___."

Answer and explanation

A. causal effect

Self-selection and unmeasured differences prevent this comparison from isolating causation.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Another round with an AI partner

Copy a finished AI prompt for this lesson

These prompts are fully written and include this lesson's facts and language. Open one, copy it as written, and paste it into a new AI chat.

Build vocabulary and collocations — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

REFERENCE
Course: Higher Education and Research English
Professional audience: faculty, postdoctoral researchers, graduate students, lab managers, research administrators, grant staff, ethics-board coordinators, and academic program leaders
Scope: All cases and figures are fictional. This book teaches research communication, not statistical, clinical, or legal advice or ethics approval. Follow the relevant institution, funder, jurisdiction, and journal requirements; obtain required review before conducting or changing research.
Lesson 01: Research Questions and Study Design
Original fictional case: Doctoral researcher Imani and supervisor Leon revise a proposal asking whether optional tutoring works. Available records cover first-year students in one introductory biology course for one semester: 32 of 40 tutoring participants completed the course, compared with 36 of 60 nonparticipants. Tutoring was self-selected, not randomly assigned. The groups may differ in prior preparation or motivation, which these records do not measure. The immediate analysis can describe the association with completion in this cohort. A future causal study would need a suitable design; it has not been conducted.
Language workshop: What exactly would it mean for tutoring to work?
Communication goal: Specify a research question and distinguish an observed association from a causal effect.
Language guidance: 32/40 is 80%; 36/60 is 60%. The difference is 20 percentage points. Relative to 60%, the difference is approximately 33.3%, not 20%. Neither calculation removes the design limitation created by self-selection.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): "What outcome would count as working?" / "The records cover first-year students in this course." / "The observation window is one semester." / "We are measuring course completion." / "Tutoring participation was optional." / "Thirty-two of forty participants completed, or 80%." / "Thirty-six of sixty nonparticipants completed, or 60%." / "That is a 20-percentage-point difference." / "This is an observed association, not an isolated causal effect." / "Students chose whether to participate." / "Prior preparation and motivation are not measured here." / "Those are possible group differences, not confirmed causes." / "The current result concerns this course cohort." / "A causal question needs a suitable future design." / "The descriptive contrast remains worth reporting." / "The proposal should match its question to the available evidence."
Vocabulary: research question: A specific question a study is designed to answer. / target population: The group to which a research question refers. / study sample: The observed group included in the study. / cohort: A defined group observed over a specified period. / outcome variable: The measured result of interest. / exposure: A condition or experience whose relationship with an outcome is studied. / comparison group: The group used as a reference for a contrast. / observational study: A study observing conditions without assigning the exposure. / self-selection: Entry into a group through participants' own choices. / random assignment: Allocation to study conditions using a random process. / random sampling: Selection of units from a population by a random process. / association: An observed relationship between variables. / causal effect: A change attributable to an intervention or exposure. / confounder: A factor influencing both exposure and outcome that can distort their apparent causal relationship. / baseline difference: A group difference present before the studied exposure or outcome. / operational definition: A precise specification of how a concept is measured. / completion rate: The proportion meeting the defined completion outcome. / denominator: The base count used in a proportion. / percentage-point difference: The arithmetic difference between percentages. / relative difference: A difference expressed in relation to a reference value. / internal validity: The credibility of the study's inference within its setting. / external validity: The extent to which findings apply beyond the studied setting. / estimand: The precise quantity a study seeks to estimate. / study protocol: A documented plan for the study's conduct and analysis.
Speaking task: Read turns 5-8, stressing each denominator and the words percentage points. Repeat turns 9-14, distinguishing self-selection, random sampling, and random assignment. Switch roles for the transfer and read the three completed calculations with their explanations.
Partner: Imani: Doctoral researcher / Leon: Research supervisor
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: In a self-selected workshop cohort, 18 of 30 attendees and 20 of 40 nonattendees completed a task. Prior experience was not measured. The records cover one course only.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Leon: Let us keep the causal question for the next study. For this paper, can you put the course, semester, and two counts into the abstract?
Imani: The future causal effect question needs a suitable design and explicit assumptions. We should discuss that design as a proposed next step, not imply that it has already been implemented.
Leon: Revise the objective to describe completion by tutoring participation in this cohort. Then make the design limitation visible in the abstract as well.
Imani: I will update the study protocol and the proposal wording together. The useful result is a bounded comparison with clear counts, while the causal and broader-population questions remain open.
END REFERENCE
Practice grammar in context — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

REFERENCE
Course: Higher Education and Research English
Professional audience: faculty, postdoctoral researchers, graduate students, lab managers, research administrators, grant staff, ethics-board coordinators, and academic program leaders
Scope: All cases and figures are fictional. This book teaches research communication, not statistical, clinical, or legal advice or ethics approval. Follow the relevant institution, funder, jurisdiction, and journal requirements; obtain required review before conducting or changing research.
Lesson 01: Research Questions and Study Design
Original fictional case: Doctoral researcher Imani and supervisor Leon revise a proposal asking whether optional tutoring works. Available records cover first-year students in one introductory biology course for one semester: 32 of 40 tutoring participants completed the course, compared with 36 of 60 nonparticipants. Tutoring was self-selected, not randomly assigned. The groups may differ in prior preparation or motivation, which these records do not measure. The immediate analysis can describe the association with completion in this cohort. A future causal study would need a suitable design; it has not been conducted.
Language workshop: What exactly would it mean for tutoring to work?
Communication goal: Specify a research question and distinguish an observed association from a causal effect.
Language guidance: 32/40 is 80%; 36/60 is 60%. The difference is 20 percentage points. Relative to 60%, the difference is approximately 33.3%, not 20%. Neither calculation removes the design limitation created by self-selection.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): "What outcome would count as working?" / "The records cover first-year students in this course." / "The observation window is one semester." / "We are measuring course completion." / "Tutoring participation was optional." / "Thirty-two of forty participants completed, or 80%." / "Thirty-six of sixty nonparticipants completed, or 60%." / "That is a 20-percentage-point difference." / "This is an observed association, not an isolated causal effect." / "Students chose whether to participate." / "Prior preparation and motivation are not measured here." / "Those are possible group differences, not confirmed causes." / "The current result concerns this course cohort." / "A causal question needs a suitable future design." / "The descriptive contrast remains worth reporting." / "The proposal should match its question to the available evidence."
Vocabulary: research question: A specific question a study is designed to answer. / target population: The group to which a research question refers. / study sample: The observed group included in the study. / cohort: A defined group observed over a specified period. / outcome variable: The measured result of interest. / exposure: A condition or experience whose relationship with an outcome is studied. / comparison group: The group used as a reference for a contrast. / observational study: A study observing conditions without assigning the exposure. / self-selection: Entry into a group through participants' own choices. / random assignment: Allocation to study conditions using a random process. / random sampling: Selection of units from a population by a random process. / association: An observed relationship between variables. / causal effect: A change attributable to an intervention or exposure. / confounder: A factor influencing both exposure and outcome that can distort their apparent causal relationship. / baseline difference: A group difference present before the studied exposure or outcome. / operational definition: A precise specification of how a concept is measured. / completion rate: The proportion meeting the defined completion outcome. / denominator: The base count used in a proportion. / percentage-point difference: The arithmetic difference between percentages. / relative difference: A difference expressed in relation to a reference value. / internal validity: The credibility of the study's inference within its setting. / external validity: The extent to which findings apply beyond the studied setting. / estimand: The precise quantity a study seeks to estimate. / study protocol: A documented plan for the study's conduct and analysis.
Speaking task: Read turns 5-8, stressing each denominator and the words percentage points. Repeat turns 9-14, distinguishing self-selection, random sampling, and random assignment. Switch roles for the transfer and read the three completed calculations with their explanations.
Partner: Imani: Doctoral researcher / Leon: Research supervisor
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: In a self-selected workshop cohort, 18 of 30 attendees and 20 of 40 nonattendees completed a task. Prior experience was not measured. The records cover one course only.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Leon: Let us keep the causal question for the next study. For this paper, can you put the course, semester, and two counts into the abstract?
Imani: The future causal effect question needs a suitable design and explicit assumptions. We should discuss that design as a proposed next step, not imply that it has already been implemented.
Leon: Revise the objective to describe completion by tutoring participation in this cohort. Then make the design limitation visible in the abstract as well.
Imani: I will update the study protocol and the proposal wording together. The useful result is a bounded comparison with clear counts, while the causal and broader-population questions remain open.
END REFERENCE
Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

02Grant Proposals and Specific AimsA feasibility pilot cannot promise a definitive answer+

Your goal

Align a grant aim with pilot scope and interpret predefined feasibility milestones.

This lesson in the book, page 13 ↗

A Read the situation

A feasibility pilot cannot promise a definitive answer

Research lead Sora and grants adviser Ben revise a proposal for a small learning-study pilot. The draft promises to establish that a new workshop improves academic attainment. The actual pilot tests recruitment and follow-up procedures, not efficacy. A completed preparatory pilot recruited 30 students in four weeks and retained 24 at follow-up. The team had specified two local progression criteria: recruit at least 30 in four weeks and retain at least 85% of those recruited. Both must be met for automatic progression to the next planning stage. Only recruitment passed; a redesign or explicit review is needed.

Sora: Research lead / Ben: Grants adviser

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What is the pilot designed to assess?

Answer and explanation

B. Recruitment and follow-up feasibility

The brief defines the pilot's scope as procedures, not efficacy or universal conclusions.

2. What was the observed retention rate?

Answer and explanation

B. 80%

Twenty-four retained students out of thirty recruited equals eighty percent.

3. Did both local progression criteria pass?

Answer and explanation

A. No; recruitment passed and retention did not.

Recruitment met its threshold, but eighty-percent retention fell below the specified eighty-five percent.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

24/30 is 80% retention, below the local 85% criterion. With 30 recruited participants, at least 26 retained would reach or exceed 85%; 25 would be only about 83.3%. Do not round a failed threshold into a pass.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
specific aim
A focused objective a proposed study will address.refine a specific aim
long-term goal
The broader result a research program ultimately seeks.state the long-term goal
pilot study
A small-scale study testing procedures for later work.design a feasibility pilot
feasibility
Whether proposed methods can be carried out practicably.assess feasibility
efficacy
The effect of an intervention under the relevant controlled conditions.distinguish efficacy from feasibility
recruitment
Bringing eligible participants into a study.monitor recruitment
recruitment window
The period allowed for enrolling participants.define the recruitment window
retention
Continued participation through a defined study point.calculate follow-up retention
follow-up
A later observation or contact after the initial stage.complete follow-up
attrition
Loss of participants from later study observation.report participant attrition
progression criterion
A predefined condition for moving to a next stage.apply progression criteria
milestone
A specified achievement used to assess progress.set a measurable milestone
deliverable
A defined output the project will provide.identify the pilot deliverable
study objective
The particular purpose of a study.align the study objective
rationale
The reasoning that justifies the proposed work.strengthen the study rationale
significance
Why the research question or gap matters.explain research significance
innovation
A meaningful new feature of an approach.describe the innovation precisely
approach
The planned methods for addressing the aims.evaluate the proposed approach
scope
The boundaries of the work and claims.keep the aims within scope
sample size
The number of units or participants included.justify the sample size
precision
The degree of uncertainty around an estimate.discuss estimate precision
definitive study
A study designed to provide a robust answer to the main question.plan a definitive study
contingency
A planned response if assumptions or milestones fail.specify a contingency
funding decision
A determination about financial support.distinguish a funding decision

The two criteria are conjunctive: both are required for automatic progression. Recruitment success does not cancel the retention shortfall. Failing this local rule calls for redesign or review; it does not prove the workshop has no educational effect.

C Notice the language

Ambition and deliverables are different

A proposal can explain a significant long-term problem without promising that a small pilot will solve it. State the pilot deliverable precisely and show how it informs a later study. Credible limits help reviewers understand what their funding would actually produce.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Separate ambition from aim
"The long-term goal is broader than this pilot deliverable."
State the pilot purpose
"This study tests recruitment and follow-up procedures."
Remove an overclaim
"It is not designed to establish efficacy."
Name the recruitment target
"The local target was 30 students in four weeks."
Report recruitment
"That target was met."
State the retention base
"Retention is calculated among the 30 recruited students."
Give the result
"Twenty-four were retained, which is 80%."
Apply the threshold
"That is below the predefined 85% criterion."
Keep both conditions
"Automatic progression requires both criteria."
Avoid convenient rounding
"Eighty percent cannot be reported as meeting 85%."
Explain the next step
"The shortfall needs redesign or explicit review."
Preserve uncertainty
"This does not establish that the workshop is ineffective."
Specify the output
"The deliverable is a revised, tested study procedure."
Retain significance
"The larger educational question remains important."
Bound funding language
"Meeting a local milestone would not guarantee an award."
Close the aim
"The proposal should promise what this design can deliver."
6 language and precision notes
Pilot
State whether it tests feasibility rather than definitive effects.
At least
Includes the threshold and values above it.
Retained
Needs a specified follow-up point and denominator.
Both
One successful criterion is insufficient.
Automatic progression
A local planning rule, not a funding guarantee.
Did not meet
Does not establish that the intervention has no effect.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which specific aim matches the supplied pilot?

Answer and explanation

B. Assess recruitment and follow-up procedures to refine a later study.

Recruitment and follow-up procedures are the pilot's actual focus; retention is not an attainment measure, and both stated criteria still apply.

2. How many retained participants would meet at least 85% of thirty?

Answer and explanation

D. Twenty-six

Twenty-six out of thirty exceeds eighty-five percent, whereas twenty-five is below it.

3. What is the correct progression statement?

Answer and explanation

C. Recruitment passed, but retention requires redesign or explicit review before progression.

Both criteria were required, and the retention shortfall prevents automatic progression under that rule.

4. What does the retention result say about efficacy?

Answer and explanation

C. It does not establish whether the workshop improves attainment.

Retention describes follow-up feasibility, not the workshop's effect on academic attainment.

Check explanations in the book, page 87 ↗

E Conversations

A feasibility pilot cannot promise a definitive answer

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Sora: Research lead / Ben: Grants adviser

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • scope
  • specific aim
  • progression criterion
  • deliverable
  • retention
  • contingency
  • Feasibility
  • recruitment
  • funding decision
  • rationale
  1. Ben
    The draft says this pilot will establish that the workshop improves attainment. Is that really the question the small study was designed to answer?
  2. Sora
    No. The 1 should concern recruitment and follow-up procedures. Improving attainment is our long-term goal, but the pilot does not provide a definitive test of that outcome.
  3. Ben
    We can keep the wider educational problem in the significance section. Reviewers still need a concrete account of what this grant would deliver now.
  4. Sora
    The 2 is a refined study procedure, informed by recruitment and follow-up evidence. That is useful preparation for a later study, without pretending the larger question has already been answered.
  5. Ben
    The preparatory work enrolled thirty students in four weeks. Remind me whether that matches the target set before the results were available.
  6. Sora
    It meets the 3 criterion of at least thirty within four weeks. We should report that success, while checking the other criterion separately instead of treating enrollment as the whole feasibility assessment.
  7. Ben
    Can we replace good retention with the actual rate? We kept twenty-four of thirty, and the milestone table still says eighty-five percent.
  8. Sora
    The 4 rate is eighty percent. We had set at least eighty-five percent, so this criterion was not met. The denominator is all thirty recruited students.
  9. Ben
    Would twenty-five retained students meet the threshold? We need to translate the percentage into a whole-participant count.
  10. Sora
    The 5 requires at least twenty-six retained out of thirty. Twenty-five would be approximately eighty-three point three percent, still below the threshold. We should apply the stated rule accurately.
  11. Ben
    Then the milestone table needs one pass and one shortfall. I cannot sign off automatic progression on the recruitment number alone.
  12. Sora
    We need a 6 such as redesign or explicit review. We should explain what the shortfall means for planning, not silently lower the criterion because we prefer a positive result.
  13. Ben
    That does not mean the workshop is educationally ineffective. The missing follow-up information concerns the study procedure, and the pilot was not an efficacy trial.
  14. Sora
    Correct. 7 and efficacy are different questions. The result tells us the retention procedure missed our local target; it does not establish whether the workshop improves attainment.
  15. Ben
    I will also remove the sentence promising a future award once the pilot milestones pass. Our internal progression rule cannot bind a funder's decision.
  16. Sora
    A 8 remains separate. Even successful local milestones would inform a proposal, not guarantee financial support. We should state the value of the evidence without predicting an external decision.
  17. Ben
    The grant can remain ambitious: a clear problem, a bounded pilot, and a reasoned route toward a definitive study.
  18. Sora
    Yes. Keep the 9 explicit. We can describe the future question and explain how improving follow-up supports it, while keeping the current work focused on procedures and practical uncertainty.
  19. Ben
    I will revise the aims, summary, and milestone table together. They should all say recruitment passed, retention did not, and the next planning step requires review.
  20. Sora
    That gives us a consistent 10 for the proposed work. We are asking for support to resolve a concrete procedural problem, not claiming that a small pilot has settled the educational effect.

Answers and explanations
  1. specific aim Turn 2. The specific aim must describe the work this study can address, which is procedural feasibility rather than definitive attainment improvement.
  2. deliverable Turn 4. The deliverable is the practical output of the pilot, here a refined and tested procedure for later research.
  3. recruitment Turn 6. Recruitment concerns bringing students into the study; thirty within four weeks meets the supplied local recruitment threshold.
  4. retention Turn 8. Retention is twenty-four of thirty recruited students, or eighty percent, at the specified follow-up point.
  5. progression criterion Turn 10. The progression criterion requires at least eighty-five percent, which with thirty recruited students needs at least twenty-six retained.
  6. contingency Turn 12. A contingency addresses the failed milestone through redesign or explicit review rather than ignoring or silently changing the predefined rule.
  7. Feasibility Turn 14. Feasibility concerns whether the proposed methods can be carried out, which is distinct from the intervention's effect on attainment.
  8. funding decision Turn 16. A funding decision is made by the relevant funder and is not guaranteed by satisfying an internal planning milestone.
  9. scope Turn 18. Scope defines what the pilot will do and claim, preventing the long-term research ambition from becoming an unsupported present promise.
  10. rationale Turn 20. The rationale explains why the bounded pilot work remains valuable while acknowledging the evidence and unresolved milestone.
Read the complete conversation

Dialogue in the book, page 19 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

A pilot can test feasibility, not solve the whole field

Fictional grant review using the lesson case: a small pilot promises to solve a broad scientific problem.

  1. Grant reviewer: The draft promises to solve the broad problem. What can this small pilot actually establish?
  2. Principal investigator: It can examine feasibility of our proposed approach, but it cannot resolve the entire scientific question.
  3. Grant reviewer: Then the specific aims should distinguish what you will assess from the long-term significance.
  4. Principal investigator: Agreed. The broader motivation belongs in the rationale, not as a guaranteed result of the pilot.
  5. Grant reviewer: Do you already have evidence that recruitment and measurement will work as planned?
  6. Principal investigator: Not yet. Those are feasibility questions for the proposed study, not completed findings.
  7. Grant reviewer: Keep innovation separate too. A novel approach isn't automatically an effective one.
  8. Principal investigator: I'll describe what is new and what the pilot would test without claiming success in advance.
  9. Grant reviewer: That can still be persuasive if the aims are bounded and the limitations are explicit.
  10. Principal investigator: I'll revise the promise into a feasible assessment and retain the broader problem as motivation.
A promised recruitment rate has no pilot evidence

Fictional grant planning: a team proposes recruiting 30 participants in a month but has no recruitment-rate data or confirmed access arrangement.

  1. Research administrator: The grant says thirty participants will enroll in a month. What supports that feasibility claim?
  2. Principal investigator: It is a planning assumption. We don't have recruitment-rate evidence or a confirmed access arrangement yet.
  3. Research administrator: Then distinguish the proposed target from a guaranteed enrollment result in the specific aims.
  4. Principal investigator: Could the pilot itself assess recruitment feasibility rather than assume the target is already achievable?
  5. Research administrator: Yes, if the design and wording make that exploratory purpose clear and preserve required approvals.
  6. Principal investigator: I'll ask the recruitment partner about access without presenting their involvement as committed before confirmation.
  7. Research administrator: Good. A letter request isn't a completed partnership, and interest isn't verified recruitment capacity.
  8. Principal investigator: The significance section can explain why the population matters without exaggerating our access to it.
  9. Research administrator: Exactly. Reviewers should see the current evidence, the proposed work, and the uncertainties separately.
  10. Principal investigator: I'll revise the feasibility language and identify recruitment access as an unresolved condition in the plan.
Innovation does not guarantee superior performance

Fictional proposal review: a researcher calls a new measurement approach more accurate, although no comparison with the existing method has been completed.

  1. Grant-development officer: The draft calls the new method more accurate. Have you completed the comparison supporting that claim?
  2. Researcher: No. We have a new approach, but its accuracy relative to the existing method hasn't been evaluated.
  3. Grant-development officer: Then novelty supports an innovation description, not a completed superiority finding.
  4. Researcher: Could one specific aim compare agreement and error under defined conditions instead of promising better performance?
  5. Grant-development officer: That is more bounded. The proposed evaluation criteria still need to be justified in the methodology.
  6. Researcher: I'll describe the expected benefit as a hypothesis and distinguish it from what we've already observed.
  7. Grant-development officer: Please also explain the significance of learning that the method does not outperform the existing one.
  8. Researcher: That result could still inform method selection, though I shouldn't claim any outcome before the study.
  9. Grant-development officer: Exactly. A credible aim promises a relevant investigation, not the favorable answer reviewers might hope for.
  10. Researcher: I'll revise the proposal so innovation, feasibility, and untested performance expectations remain clearly separated.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

A feasibility pilot cannot promise a definitive answer
  1. Read turns 5-12, stating 24 out of 30 and the minimum qualifying count of 26 clearly.
  2. Repeat turns 13-16, separating feasibility, efficacy, and the funding decision.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Two conditions, not one

A local pilot rule requires 20 recruits within three weeks and at least 90% retention. Twenty are recruited on time, but only 17 reach follow-up. Both conditions are needed for automatic progression.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Researcher: "The recruitment condition was ___."

Answer and explanation

C. met

Twenty students were recruited within the stated three-week window.

2. Adviser: "Retention was ___ percent."

Answer and explanation

D. 85

Seventeen divided by twenty equals eighty-five percent, below ninety percent.

3. Researcher: "At least ___ retained students were required."

Answer and explanation

C. 18

Eighteen out of twenty equals the required ninety-percent retention threshold.

4. Adviser: "Automatic progression is ___ under the stated rule."

Answer and explanation

A. not supported

Only recruitment passed, while the rule requires both conditions to be satisfied.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary

Lesson 02: Grant Proposals and Specific Aims
Original fictional case: Research lead Sora and grants adviser Ben revise a proposal for a small learning-study pilot. The draft promises to establish that a new workshop improves academic attainment. The actual pilot tests recruitment and follow-up procedures, not efficacy. A completed preparatory pilot recruited 30 students in four weeks and retained 24 at follow-up. The team had specified two local progression criteria: recruit at least 30 in four weeks and retain at least 85% of those recruited. Both must be met for automatic progression to the next planning stage. Only recruitment passed; a redesign or explicit review is needed.
Language workshop: A feasibility pilot cannot promise a definitive answer
Communication goal: Align a grant aim with pilot scope and interpret predefined feasibility milestones.
Language guidance: 24/30 is 80% retention, below the local 85% criterion. With 30 recruited participants, at least 26 retained would reach or exceed 85%; 25 would be only about 83.3%. Do not round a failed threshold into a pass.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): "The long-term goal is broader than this pilot deliverable." / "This study tests recruitment and follow-up procedures." / "It is not designed to establish efficacy." / "The local target was 30 students in four weeks." / "That target was met." / "Retention is calculated among the 30 recruited students." / "Twenty-four were retained, which is 80%." / "That is below the predefined 85% criterion." / "Automatic progression requires both criteria." / "Eighty percent cannot be reported as meeting 85%." / "The shortfall needs redesign or explicit review." / "This does not establish that the workshop is ineffective." / "The deliverable is a revised, tested study procedure." / "The larger educational question remains important." / "Meeting a local milestone would not guarantee an award." / "The proposal should promise what this design can deliver."
Vocabulary: specific aim: A focused objective a proposed study will address. / long-term goal: The broader result a research program ultimately seeks. / pilot study: A small-scale study testing procedures for later work. / feasibility: Whether proposed methods can be carried out practicably. / efficacy: The effect of an intervention under the relevant controlled conditions. / recruitment: Bringing eligible participants into a study. / recruitment window: The period allowed for enrolling participants. / retention: Continued participation through a defined study point. / follow-up: A later observation or contact after the initial stage. / attrition: Loss of participants from later study observation. / progression criterion: A predefined condition for moving to a next stage. / milestone: A specified achievement used to assess progress. / deliverable: A defined output the project will provide. / study objective: The particular purpose of a study. / rationale: The reasoning that justifies the proposed work. / significance: Why the research question or gap matters. / innovation: A meaningful new feature of an approach. / approach: The planned methods for addressing the aims. / scope: The boundaries of the work and claims. / sample size: The number of units or participants included. / precision: The degree of uncertainty around an estimate. / definitive study: A study designed to provide a robust answer to the main question. / contingency: A planned response if assumptions or milestones fail. / funding decision: A determination about financial support.
Speaking task: Read turns 5-12, stating 24 out of 30 and the minimum qualifying count of 26 clearly. Repeat turns 13-16, separating feasibility, efficacy, and the funding decision. Switch roles for the transfer; read the 85% observation and 90% requirement without rounding one into the other.
Partner: Sora: Research lead / Ben: Grants adviser
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: A local pilot rule requires 20 recruits within three weeks and at least 90% retention. Twenty are recruited on time, but only 17 reach follow-up. Both conditions are needed for automatic progression.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Ben: The grant can remain ambitious: a clear problem, a bounded pilot, and a reasoned route toward a definitive study.
Sora: Yes. Keep the scope explicit. We can describe the future question and explain how improving follow-up supports it, while keeping the current work focused on procedures and practical uncertainty.
Ben: I will revise the aims, summary, and milestone table together. They should all say recruitment passed, retention did not, and the next planning step requires review.
Sora: That gives us a consistent rationale for the proposed work. We are asking for support to resolve a concrete procedural problem, not claiming that a small pilot has settled the educational effect.
END REFERENCE

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.
