03Lab Meetings and Data ChallengesThe repeated run was not performed the same way+

Your goal

Present an experimental discrepancy, disclose a method change, and propose a controlled check without editing the record to fit a preferred result.

This lesson in the book, page 22 ↗

A Read the situation

The repeated run was not performed the same way

Research associate Alex and lab lead Priya compare two nonclinical fluorescence measurements of the same reference material. Run A reports 120 arbitrary units and Run B 180. Instrument files reveal exposure times of ten seconds for A and twenty for B. The summary lab notes had omitted this change. The team has not tested whether it explains the entire difference, and signal response may not be proportional to exposure. They must retain both raw records, add a dated correction to the notes, and plan a repeat with documented matched settings. No misconduct finding has been made.

Alex: Research associate / Priya: Lab lead

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What changed between the runs?

Answer and explanation

D. Exposure time increased from ten to twenty seconds.

Instrument files establish different exposure times for the two recorded measurements.

2. What is not yet established?

Answer and explanation

C. Whether exposure time explains the entire signal difference

The method difference is known, but its full contribution to the result has not been tested.

3. How should the documentation be corrected?

Answer and explanation

D. Add a dated correction while preserving the original record.

Transparent correction preserves provenance without concealing the original omission or inventing a misconduct finding.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Run B is 60 arbitrary units higher, a 50% increase relative to Run A. Exposure doubled, but the signal increased by only half. Do not divide or scale signals as though proportional response had been established.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
lab meeting
A discussion of research progress, methods, and results.present at a lab meeting
experimental run
A recorded execution of a measurement or experiment.compare experimental runs
reference material
Material used as a stable point of comparison.measure the reference material
raw data
Original recorded observations before later processing.preserve raw data
processed result
A value produced after defined analysis or transformation.trace a processed result
arbitrary unit
A relative measurement unit defined by the measurement system.report arbitrary units
exposure time
The duration used for signal acquisition in this case.document exposure time
instrument setting
A parameter controlling measurement behavior.match instrument settings
protocol version
The identified revision of a study procedure.record the protocol version
method deviation
A departure from the intended or comparison procedure.document a method deviation
batch effect
A systematic difference associated with processing groups.investigate a batch effect
technical replicate
A repeated measurement used to assess measurement variability.compare technical replicates
biological replicate
An independently derived biological sample or unit.distinguish biological replicates
signal intensity
The magnitude of the detected measurement signal.compare signal intensity
linear response
A straight-line input-output relationship over a specified range, possibly with a nonzero offset.verify a linear response
saturation
A measurement ceiling beyond which more input is not represented proportionally.check for signal saturation
normalization
Adjustment to a defined reference or scale.justify normalization
measurement variability
Variation arising across repeated measurements.quantify measurement variability
controlled comparison
A comparison with relevant conditions deliberately held consistent.plan a controlled comparison
lab notebook
The record of research methods, observations, and changes.maintain the lab notebook
dated correction
A visible amendment recording when a correction was made.add a dated correction
provenance
The origin and history of data or records.preserve data provenance
hypothesis
A proposed explanation or prediction to be tested.test a methodological hypothesis
repeatability check
A check of measurement agreement under the same specified short-term conditions.document a repeatability check

A recorded method difference is a candidate explanation, not proof of the entire cause. Retain both results and document the correction. Repeating measurements of the same material does not create new independent biological samples.

C Notice the language

Disclose a discrepancy without assigning blame

A lab meeting should make uncertainty inspectable. Name the observed result, the method difference, and the missing evidence. Correct documentation transparently without accusing a colleague of misconduct or presenting an untested explanation as the established cause.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Open with the result
"Run A gave 120 units; Run B gave 180."
Name the verified change
"The instrument files show different exposure times."
State the durations
"A used ten seconds and B used twenty."
Disclose the omission
"The summary notes did not record that change."
Preserve the records
"Keep both raw files and their original settings."
Correct transparently
"Add a dated correction to the notebook."
Avoid blame
"This is a documentation gap, not a misconduct finding."
Limit the explanation
"The change may contribute, but we have not isolated its effect."
Question a shortcut
"Proportional scaling has not been validated."
Check the response
"We need to examine linearity and possible saturation."
Plan the comparison
"Repeat with documented matched settings."
Keep replication precise
"These are repeated measurements of the same material."
Track processing
"State every transformation applied to the raw values."
Separate observation and cause
"The signal changed; the complete cause is unresolved."
Make the action traceable
"Link the repeat to the protocol version and instrument files."
Close honestly
"We have a testable explanation, not a resolved discrepancy."
6 language and precision notes
Repeated
Does not guarantee identical conditions.
Raw
Keep the original record separate from later transformations.
May explain
A hypothesis, not a completed causal test.
Twice the exposure
Does not establish twice the signal.
Correction
Must remain distinguishable from the original entry.
Independent sample
Not created by measuring the same material again.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which sentence accurately reports the signal contrast?

Answer and explanation

C. Run B is 60 units, or 50%, higher than Run A.

One hundred eighty minus one hundred twenty is sixty, which is half of the original value.

2. Why is dividing Run B by two not justified as a correction?

Answer and explanation

C. Proportional signal response to exposure has not been established.

Scaling assumes a relationship that the brief explicitly says has not been verified.

3. What is the next useful comparison?

Answer and explanation

B. A documented repeat with matched settings and preserved raw records

Matching and documenting conditions helps examine the discrepancy without concealing existing evidence.

4. Which conclusion should be avoided?

Answer and explanation

C. The omitted note proves research misconduct.

An omission requires examination and correction but does not by itself establish a misconduct finding.

Check explanations in the book, page 89 ↗

E Conversations

The repeated run was not performed the same way

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Alex: Research associate / Priya: Lab lead

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • provenance
  • dated correction
  • controlled comparison
  • technical replicates
  • saturation
  • instrument setting
  • hypothesis
  • raw data
  • repeatability check
  • linear response
  1. Priya
    The slide calls Run B a repeat, but its signal is one hundred eighty units rather than one hundred twenty. Were the measurement conditions actually matched?
  2. Alex
    The 1 for exposure changed. The files show ten seconds for Run A and twenty for Run B. That difference was omitted from the summary notes.
  3. Priya
    Preserve both files, including the unexpected result, before correcting the notes. We need the original evidence intact.
  4. Alex
    I have retained the 2 and the original metadata. I will not replace either measurement with an adjusted value. Any later processing must remain distinguishable from what the instrument originally recorded.
  5. Priya
    The exposure doubled, so somebody suggested halving the second signal and treating that as the comparable result. Have we established a proportional response?
  6. Alex
    No. A 3 has not been verified here. Even a straight-line response could include background signal. We cannot simply halve one raw value and call it corrected.
  7. Priya
    Check whether the detector reaches a ceiling or other conditions differed. The exposure change is a lead, not a complete explanation.
  8. Alex
    We can check for 4 and review other recorded conditions. I will describe these as possible contributors, not confirmed causes. We have not isolated the exposure effect or explained the full discrepancy.
  9. Priya
    How will the correction show when we discovered the omitted setting, without hiding the original note?
  10. Alex
    I will add a 5 linked to the instrument files. It will state the actual exposure for each run and when the omission was identified, without backdating the new entry.
  11. Priya
    Good. That also avoids implying misconduct before anything has been investigated. The immediate issue is an incomplete method record and a result needing explanation.
  12. Alex
    Preserving 6 keeps the measurement, original notes, and later correction connected. I will record what changed and when, without assigning blame before the facts are checked.
  13. Priya
    For the next measurement, we need documented matched settings and a clear plan. Simply running the instrument again would not address the comparison problem.
  14. Alex
    I will propose a 7 using matched settings and retained raw files. We should specify the protocol version and checks in advance, so another undocumented change does not create the same uncertainty.
  15. Priya
    For that repeat, keep the operator, instrument, location, and procedure the same within one session. We are testing measurement consistency, not collecting new biological samples.
  16. Alex
    Those planned measurements are 8. The original exposure settings differed, so those two runs are not a matched set. Neither repeating the measurement nor changing exposure adds an independent biological sample.
  17. Priya
    The slide should report the observed difference as sixty units, fifty percent relative to Run A. It must not say exposure caused exactly that increase.
  18. Alex
    I will separate the observation from the 9. The documented change is a plausible contributor. Its role remains to be tested, and other sources of variation have not been ruled out.
  19. Priya
    Bring both raw files, the corrected record, and the matched-settings plan next time. We can then review the evidence together.
  20. Alex
    I will label the planned work a 10. The two original runs and their settings stay in the record. We still need to explain the discrepancy; scheduling a repeat does not close it.

Answers and explanations
  1. instrument setting Turn 2. The instrument setting difference is established by the files: exposure was ten seconds in one run and twenty in the other.
  2. raw data Turn 4. Raw data preserves the original observations and settings, allowing later checks without losing an inconvenient result.
  3. linear response Turn 6. A linear response can include an offset; proportional rescaling also requires a justified zero or background correction and a validated range.
  4. saturation Turn 8. Saturation could make the signal response nonproportional, but it is only a possibility to investigate in this case.
  5. dated correction Turn 10. A dated correction records the discovery transparently while preserving the original note and the history of the omission.
  6. provenance Turn 12. Provenance records the origin and history of the data and corrections, making the discrepancy traceable without prejudging responsibility.
  7. controlled comparison Turn 14. A controlled comparison holds relevant settings consistent so the team can investigate the discrepancy more meaningfully.
  8. technical replicates Turn 16. The planned matched measurements assess measurement variability on the same material, not additional independent biological samples.
  9. hypothesis Turn 18. The hypothesis proposes that the method change contributes to the discrepancy, but it still requires testing rather than being reported as established.
  10. repeatability check Turn 20. The same operator, instrument, method, location, and session specify repeatability conditions, unlike a changed-condition measurement reproducibility study.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Priya: The slide calls Run B a repeat, but its signal is one hundred eighty units rather than one hundred twenty. Were the measurement conditions actually matched?
  2. Alex: The instrument setting for exposure changed. The files show ten seconds for Run A and twenty for Run B. That difference was omitted from the summary notes.
  3. Priya: Preserve both files, including the unexpected result, before correcting the notes. We need the original evidence intact.
  4. Alex: I have retained the raw data and the original metadata. I will not replace either measurement with an adjusted value. Any later processing must remain distinguishable from what the instrument originally recorded.
  5. Priya: The exposure doubled, so somebody suggested halving the second signal and treating that as the comparable result. Have we established a proportional response?
  6. Alex: No. A linear response has not been verified here. Even a straight-line response could include background signal. We cannot simply halve one raw value and call it corrected.
  7. Priya: Check whether the detector reaches a ceiling or other conditions differed. The exposure change is a lead, not a complete explanation.
  8. Alex: We can check for saturation and review other recorded conditions. I will describe these as possible contributors, not confirmed causes. We have not isolated the exposure effect or explained the full discrepancy.
  9. Priya: How will the correction show when we discovered the omitted setting, without hiding the original note?
  10. Alex: I will add a dated correction linked to the instrument files. It will state the actual exposure for each run and when the omission was identified, without backdating the new entry.
  11. Priya: Good. That also avoids implying misconduct before anything has been investigated. The immediate issue is an incomplete method record and a result needing explanation.
  12. Alex: Preserving provenance keeps the measurement, original notes, and later correction connected. I will record what changed and when, without assigning blame before the facts are checked.
  13. Priya: For the next measurement, we need documented matched settings and a clear plan. Simply running the instrument again would not address the comparison problem.
  14. Alex: I will propose a controlled comparison using matched settings and retained raw files. We should specify the protocol version and checks in advance, so another undocumented change does not create the same uncertainty.
  15. Priya: For that repeat, keep the operator, instrument, location, and procedure the same within one session. We are testing measurement consistency, not collecting new biological samples.
  16. Alex: Those planned measurements are technical replicates. The original exposure settings differed, so those two runs are not a matched set. Neither repeating the measurement nor changing exposure adds an independent biological sample.
  17. Priya: The slide should report the observed difference as sixty units, fifty percent relative to Run A. It must not say exposure caused exactly that increase.
  18. Alex: I will separate the observation from the hypothesis. The documented change is a plausible contributor. Its role remains to be tested, and other sources of variation have not been ruled out.
  19. Priya: Bring both raw files, the corrected record, and the matched-settings plan next time. We can then review the evidence together.
  20. Alex: I will label the planned work a repeatability check. The two original runs and their settings stay in the record. We still need to explain the discrepancy; scheduling a repeat does not close it.
Dialogue in the book, page 28 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

The replication notes omit a protocol change

Fictional lab meeting using the lesson case: a new result differs from an earlier experiment, but the notes omit a protocol change.

  1. Lab lead: The results differ. Could you provide the protocol version and the change missing from these notes?
  2. Research associate: A preparation step changed, but the shared notes don't document its details yet.
  3. Lab lead: Please recover the actual record before our comparison review rather than reconstruct it from memory alone.
  4. Research associate: Should we describe the new run as a failed replication while that information remains incomplete?
  5. Lab lead: Not yet. We need to understand comparability and any protocol drift before assigning that interpretation.
  6. Research associate: I'll identify the changed step, available control records, and any information we cannot verify.
  7. Lab lead: Thank you. Also check whether the analysis assumption used for the earlier experiment remained the same.
  8. Research associate: I'll ask the analysis owner for that confirmation and keep observations separate from possible explanations.
  9. Lab lead: Can you bring the documented differences to the next lab meeting without promising a causal explanation?
  10. Research associate: Yes. I'll provide the records and gaps so the team can plan a meaningful comparison.
A control label is not a complete control definition

Fictional lab review: two datasets use the label control, but their treatment and collection conditions are not described together.

  1. Data analyst: Both files label a group control. Could you confirm whether the underlying conditions actually match?
  2. Lab researcher: I need to check the experiment records. The shared label alone doesn't describe the full setup.
  3. Data analyst: Please provide the treatment, timing, and relevant protocol version before I combine the data.
  4. Lab researcher: Would using the label as the grouping key count as an analysis assumption worth documenting?
  5. Data analyst: Yes. Assuming equivalent controls could change the interpretation, so it needs evidence rather than an implicit merge.
  6. Lab researcher: I'll request the original conditions from the experiment owners and note any differences they confirm.
  7. Data analyst: Thank you. Don't call those differences protocol drift until we know whether they were planned.
  8. Lab researcher: Understood. A deliberate design difference and an undocumented change need different explanations in the lab meeting.
  9. Data analyst: I'll hold the combined comparison until the control definitions are reviewed and the grouping decision is justified.
  10. Lab researcher: I'll bring the source records so that decision can be made with the actual conditions in view.
An undocumented exclusion rule leaves comparability unresolved

Fictional analysis review: one run excluded incomplete records, while an earlier summary does not document its exclusion rule.

  1. Research supervisor: Your new analysis excludes incomplete records. Could you confirm how the earlier summary handled them?
  2. Research analyst: That exclusion rule isn't documented in the summary. I'll check the saved analysis and its notes.
  3. Research supervisor: Please do that before describing the difference as a replicated change in the underlying phenomenon.
  4. Research analyst: The result may reflect different analysis assumptions, but we haven't established that explanation yet.
  5. Research supervisor: Exactly. Request the original rule and preserve the current result rather than quietly forcing the totals to match.
  6. Research analyst: I'll compare record counts and exclusion decisions using the available versioned materials.
  7. Research supervisor: Can you bring the confirmed rules and any unrecoverable gaps to our next review?
  8. Research analyst: Yes. I won't invent an old rule if the saved material doesn't establish one.
  9. Research supervisor: Then we can distinguish a documented methodological difference from an unexplained result difference.
  10. Research analyst: I'll make that distinction explicit and ask the team to review any proposed reanalysis before proceeding.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

The repeated run was not performed the same way
  1. Read turns 1-10; distinguish the recorded setting change from an untested explanation.
  2. Repeat turns 13-16, stressing same session and technical replicates.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Preserve the change history

Two readings of the same reference material used different documented gain settings. The summary omitted the change. No causal check has been completed. Both original files remain available.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Researcher: "The gain change is a verified method ___."

Answer and explanation

C. difference

The instrument records establish a setting difference, not merely a suspected change.

2. Lead: "Its causal contribution remains ___."

Answer and explanation

C. untested

No check has isolated the effect of the setting change.

3. Researcher: "The notebook needs a ___."

Answer and explanation

D. dated correction

A dated correction transparently records the omission without rewriting the past.

4. Lead: "Both original files should be ___."

Answer and explanation

B. preserved

Retaining original records allows later verification and avoids selective removal of evidence.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary

3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.

REFERENCE
Course: Higher Education and Research English
Professional audience: faculty, postdoctoral researchers, graduate students, lab managers, research administrators, grant staff, ethics-board coordinators, and academic program leaders
Scope: All cases and figures are fictional. This book teaches research communication, not statistical, clinical, or legal advice or ethics approval. Follow the relevant institution, funder, jurisdiction, and journal requirements; obtain required review before conducting or changing research.
Lesson 03: Lab Meetings and Data Challenges
Original fictional case: Research associate Alex and lab lead Priya compare two nonclinical fluorescence measurements of the same reference material. Run A reports 120 arbitrary units and Run B 180. Instrument files reveal exposure times of ten seconds for A and twenty for B. The summary lab notes had omitted this change. The team has not tested whether it explains the entire difference, and signal response may not be proportional to exposure. They must retain both raw records, add a dated correction to the notes, and plan a repeat with documented matched settings. No misconduct finding has been made.
Language workshop: The repeated run was not performed the same way
Communication goal: Present an experimental discrepancy, disclose a method change, and propose a controlled check without editing the record to fit a preferred result.
Language guidance: Run B is 60 arbitrary units higher, a 50% increase relative to Run A. Exposure doubled, but the signal increased by only half. Do not divide or scale signals as though proportional response had been established.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): "Run A gave 120 units; Run B gave 180." / "The instrument files show different exposure times." / "A used ten seconds and B used twenty." / "The summary notes did not record that change." / "Keep both raw files and their original settings." / "Add a dated correction to the notebook." / "This is a documentation gap, not a misconduct finding." / "The change may contribute, but we have not isolated its effect." / "Proportional scaling has not been validated." / "We need to examine linearity and possible saturation." / "Repeat with documented matched settings." / "These are repeated measurements of the same material." / "State every transformation applied to the raw values." / "The signal changed; the complete cause is unresolved." / "Link the repeat to the protocol version and instrument files." / "We have a testable explanation, not a resolved discrepancy."
Vocabulary: lab meeting: A discussion of research progress, methods, and results. / experimental run: A recorded execution of a measurement or experiment. / reference material: Material used as a stable point of comparison. / raw data: Original recorded observations before later processing. / processed result: A value produced after defined analysis or transformation. / arbitrary unit: A relative measurement unit defined by the measurement system. / exposure time: The duration used for signal acquisition in this case. / instrument setting: A parameter controlling measurement behavior. / protocol version: The identified revision of a study procedure. / method deviation: A departure from the intended or comparison procedure. / batch effect: A systematic difference associated with processing groups. / technical replicate: A repeated measurement used to assess measurement variability. / biological replicate: An independently derived biological sample or unit. / signal intensity: The magnitude of the detected measurement signal. / linear response: A straight-line input-output relationship over a specified range, possibly with a nonzero offset. / saturation: A measurement ceiling beyond which more input is not represented proportionally. / normalization: Adjustment to a defined reference or scale. / measurement variability: Variation arising across repeated measurements. / controlled comparison: A comparison with relevant conditions deliberately held consistent. / lab notebook: The record of research methods, observations, and changes. / dated correction: A visible amendment recording when a correction was made. / provenance: The origin and history of data or records. / hypothesis: A proposed explanation or prediction to be tested. / repeatability check: A check of measurement agreement under the same specified short-term conditions.
Speaking task: Read turns 1-10; distinguish the recorded setting change from an untested explanation. Repeat turns 13-16, stressing same session and technical replicates. Switch roles for the transfer; use a firm, neutral tone for the correction without implying misconduct.
Partner: Alex: Research associate / Priya: Lab lead
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Two readings of the same reference material used different documented gain settings. The summary omitted the change. No causal check has been completed. Both original files remain available.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Priya: The slide should report the observed difference as sixty units, fifty percent relative to Run A. It must not say exposure caused exactly that increase.
Alex: I will separate the observation from the hypothesis. The documented change is a plausible contributor. Its role remains to be tested, and other sources of variation have not been ruled out.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

04Research Ethics and Human SubjectsPossessing the data is not permission for a new use+

Your goal

Describe a secondary-use proposal and seek an authorized ethics determination without assuming coded data is anonymous.

This lesson in the book, page 31 ↗

A Read the situation

Possessing the data is not permission for a new use

Researcher Tessa wants to reuse coded interviews from a student-stress study for a new employment-discrimination question. Her research team can access the key linking codes to participants. The original approval and consent materials have not been checked against the new purpose. The fictional institution requires its designated ethics office to determine the permitted route before new analysis or sharing begins. No determination, amendment, waiver, or new consent has been granted. Tessa can prepare the proposal and gather existing documents, but must not begin the proposed reuse or promise collaborators access.

Tessa: Researcher / Omar: Research ethics coordinator

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. Why are the coded interviews not automatically anonymous to this team?

Answer and explanation

C. The team can access the code-to-participant key.

The accessible linking key allows the team to connect codes with participants.

2. Who determines the permitted route under the supplied local rule?

Answer and explanation

C. The designated ethics office

The fictional institutional procedure assigns the determination to its designated ethics office.

3. What can Tessa do now?

Answer and explanation

D. Prepare the proposal and gather the existing approval and consent documents.

Preparatory documentation is permitted, while the new use and sharing must await the required determination.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Coded does not automatically mean anonymous. Here, the team can access a linking key, so the identity connection matters. The new question and access arrangements must be described accurately to the designated reviewer.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
secondary use
Using previously collected material for a further purpose.propose a secondary use
human-subjects research
Research involving people or qualifying information under applicable rules.assess human-subjects research status
ethics review
Examination of a research proposal's ethical acceptability and requirements.request ethics review
institutional review board
A U.S. committee reviewing covered human-subjects research.consult the institutional review board
research ethics committee
A body reviewing research ethics under its remit.seek research ethics committee review
informed consent
A process supporting an informed, voluntary participation decision.review informed-consent materials
consent scope
The activities and uses covered by the relevant consent.check the consent scope
approval scope
The work permitted by a particular authorization.verify the approval scope
protocol amendment
A proposed change to an approved research protocol.submit a protocol amendment
waiver
An authorized exception to a specified requirement.distinguish a requested waiver
exemption determination
An authorized decision about an applicable exemption category.obtain an exemption determination
coded data
Data using codes with a separate identity-linking key.describe coded data accurately
linking key
Information connecting study codes to identities.restrict access to the linking key
identifiability
The possibility of connecting information with a person.assess identifiability
de-identification
Removing or reducing identifying information under a specified method.document de-identification
confidentiality
Protection against unauthorized information disclosure.maintain confidentiality
data-use agreement
Terms governing permitted access and use.establish a data-use agreement
authorized determination
A decision made by the designated competent authority.await an authorized determination
recontact
Contacting a participant again after earlier involvement.assess a recontact proposal
participant information
Material explaining a study to potential participants.review participant information
voluntariness
Freedom to choose participation without improper pressure.protect voluntariness
access restriction
A limit on who may obtain or use information.apply access restrictions
purpose change
A shift from the original use to a different objective.describe the purpose change
review status
The current stage or outcome of a review request.state the review status

A request for an amendment, waiver, or exemption is not a granted determination. The applicable route depends on the study and rules. This scenario supplies a local hold on new analysis and sharing until the ethics office determines that route.

C Notice the language

A review boundary is not a scientific rejection

An ethics coordinator may support the question while withholding permission for the proposed use. Explain the purpose and access conditions accurately. Do not turn a discussion about a possible route into a claim that approval, exemption, or participant agreement has already been obtained.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Describe the new question
"The proposed reuse concerns employment discrimination."
Identify the original purpose
"The interviews were collected for a student-stress study."
State the key access
"Our team can access the code-to-participant key."
Avoid an anonymity claim
"Coding does not make these data anonymous to us."
Check the documents
"We need the original approval and consent materials."
Clarify the scope
"The new purpose has not been assessed against those documents."
Name the authority
"The designated ethics office determines the permitted route."
State the hold
"Do not start the new analysis or sharing yet."
Keep requests conditional
"An amendment may be needed; none has been approved."
Avoid inventing a waiver
"No waiver or new consent has been granted."
Prepare useful material
"We can describe the question, fields, recipients, and safeguards."
Separate preparation from reuse
"Gathering documents is not approval to analyze the interviews."
Limit a collaborator promise
"Access is subject to the authorized determination."
Preserve confidentiality
"Keep the current access restrictions in place."
Report status precisely
"The proposal is awaiting review, not approved."
Close with the next step
"Submit the complete proposal and wait for the required decision."
6 language and precision notes
Coded
A linking key may still allow identification.
Anonymous
Do not use without a justified assessment of the actual situation.
Existing approval
May not cover a new question or sharing arrangement.
May require
A possibility, not a determination already made.
Requested
Not granted.
Preparing a proposal
Distinct from conducting the proposed new research.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which description should be sent to the ethics office?

Answer and explanation

C. Coded interviews with a linking key accessible to the research team

The correct description accurately reports the team's actual ability to link codes with people.

2. What does the old approval establish about this new purpose?

Answer and explanation

D. Nothing conclusive until its scope is checked against the proposal.

The original materials have not been reviewed against the new use, so their coverage cannot be assumed.

3. Which message to a collaborator is accurate?

Answer and explanation

D. "The proposed access awaits the designated ethics determination."

The correct message states the pending status without inventing approval or participant agreement.

4. Which preparatory step respects the supplied boundary?

Answer and explanation

A. Gather documents and describe the proposed use without beginning analysis or sharing.

The local procedure permits proposal preparation while holding the proposed research use and disclosure.

E Conversations

Possessing the data is not permission for a new use

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Tessa: Researcher / Omar: Research ethics coordinator

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • authorized determination
  • data-use agreement
  • review status
  • access restriction
  • secondary use
  • consent scope
  • protocol amendment
  • coded data
  • purpose change
  • linking key
  1. Tessa
    We already have the interview files, and the new employment-discrimination question is promising. Can I begin analyzing them while we check the old paperwork?
  2. Omar
    The local 1 does not permit that yet. The designated ethics office must determine the route before new analysis or sharing. You can prepare the proposal and gather the existing documents.
  3. Tessa
    The interviews came from our student-stress study. They use participant codes rather than names, so I initially described them as anonymous in the draft.
  4. Omar
    Describe the 2 accurately. Does the research team have access to the key connecting the codes with participants? That access changes what the reviewer needs to know about identifiability.
  5. Tessa
    Yes, the team can access the key. The draft should state that plainly rather than imply there is no possible connection with the people interviewed.
  6. Omar
    Exactly. The 3 must be included in the access description. Coding alone does not establish anonymity to your team, and changing the label would not change the underlying arrangement.
  7. Tessa
    I have the original participant information and consent documents, but I have not checked whether the employment question is within what they describe.
  8. Omar
    Review the 4 alongside the original approval. We should not assume that agreement to the student-stress study covers every later question or sharing arrangement.
  9. Tessa
    I will send both research questions side by side. The employment topic is not actually named in the documents I have opened so far.
  10. Omar
    Yes, state the 5 clearly. The reviewer needs the actual new question, intended information, recipients, and safeguards, not a broad assurance that everything is still generally about students.
  11. Tessa
    Would this be an amendment, a new submission, or an exemption? Collaborators keep asking me which route applies, and I do not want to guess.
  12. Omar
    That requires an 6. The designated office will assess the proposal under the applicable rules. We cannot announce the route before that assessment has been made.
  13. Tessa
    I can say an amendment might be needed, but none is approved. The draft wrongly implies approval is routine.
  14. Omar
    Keep any 7 conditional. A plausible route is not a completed decision. Likewise, no waiver or new consent has been granted, so neither should appear as an established fact.
  15. Tessa
    The collaborator says they only want to check the available variables. Could I send a description of the proposed fields instead of the interviews?
  16. Omar
    Yes, describe the proposed fields without sharing participant information. The 8 on the interviews remains. Do not promise that the files themselves will be released before the designated review.
  17. Tessa
    I will gather the approval, consent materials, key-access details, and proposed analysis description. That gives the office enough context to assess the new use.
  18. Omar
    Include the proposed 9 arrangements if collaboration is planned. Such terms may be relevant, but they do not replace the required ethics determination or establish participant consent by themselves.
  19. Tessa
    My update will say the proposal is being prepared, no new analysis has begun, and collaborator access remains subject to the designated review.
  20. Omar
    That accurately describes the 10. Keep preparation separate from conducting the new work. Once the authorized decision is recorded, follow its conditions rather than treating review as unlimited permission.

Answers and explanations
  1. review status Turn 2. The review status is unresolved: the required determination has not been made, so the new analysis cannot start under the supplied rule.
  2. coded data Turn 4. Coded data uses substitute labels but may remain identifiable through a linking key, which this team can access.
  3. linking key Turn 6. The linking key connects the interview codes to participant identities and must be disclosed in the description of access conditions.
  4. consent scope Turn 8. Consent scope concerns the uses covered by the relevant participant materials, which have not been checked against the new purpose.
  5. purpose change Turn 10. The purpose change distinguishes the proposed employment-discrimination question from the original student-stress research and requires assessment.
  6. authorized determination Turn 12. The authorized determination is made by the designated ethics office, not inferred by collaborators or the researcher from possession of the files.
  7. protocol amendment Turn 14. A protocol amendment is a proposed change to approved work and must not be described as already authorized when none has been granted.
  8. access restriction Turn 16. The access restriction remains in force for the interviews while the determination is pending; a general description does not authorize disclosure.
  9. data-use agreement Turn 18. A data-use agreement can describe intended access and use conditions, but it does not replace the required ethics determination.
  10. secondary use Turn 20. Secondary use is the proposed reuse for a new research purpose, which remains pending rather than already permitted.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Tessa: We already have the interview files, and the new employment-discrimination question is promising. Can I begin analyzing them while we check the old paperwork?
  2. Omar: The local review status does not permit that yet. The designated ethics office must determine the route before new analysis or sharing. You can prepare the proposal and gather the existing documents.

  9. Tessa: I will send both research questions side by side. The employment topic is not actually named in the documents I have opened so far.

Dialogue in the book, page 37 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

A new question needs an approval-scope check

Fictional research-ethics discussion using the lesson case: a researcher wants secondary use of participant data before reviewing existing approval and consent materials.

  1. Researcher: Can we reuse the participant dataset for a new question since the original study was approved?
  2. Ethics coordinator: First clarify the new purpose and review the existing approval and informed-consent materials.
  3. Researcher: I haven't checked those documents. Does removing names automatically make the secondary use permissible?
  4. Ethics coordinator: No automatic conclusion follows here. The authorized ethics team needs the relevant scope and data details.
  5. Researcher: Should I ask the institutional review board, or IRB, whether an amendment or another route applies?
  6. Ethics coordinator: Use our institution's established review process and ask the responsible office which route is appropriate.
  7. Researcher: I'll describe the proposed question, requested fields, and intended access without starting the new analysis.
  8. Ethics coordinator: Good. Distinguish what the original human-subjects approval actually covers from what you hope it covers.
  9. Researcher: I'll retrieve the approved documents and mark any unclear consent provision for the office to assess.
  10. Ethics coordinator: Then the review can address the real proposed use rather than an assumption based on prior approval.
Public availability does not answer every ethics question

Fictional research planning: a team proposes studying publicly accessible forum posts; institutional review applicability and data handling have not been assessed.

  1. Graduate researcher: The forum posts are publicly accessible. Does that settle whether our study needs any ethics review?
  2. Research ethics adviser: Not by itself. Please clarify the question, collection approach, and planned handling for the responsible office.
  3. Graduate researcher: We haven't discussed whether quotations could make contributors identifiable in the final paper.
  4. Research ethics adviser: That is relevant context to raise, not a reason to make a blanket legal or ethical conclusion here.
  5. Graduate researcher: Should I begin collecting everything while we decide which details eventually matter to the study?
  6. Research ethics adviser: First establish the appropriate review route and scope. Don't assume public access authorizes every proposed research use.
  7. Graduate researcher: I'll prepare a bounded description and ask how human-subjects considerations apply to this particular proposal.
  8. Research ethics adviser: Good. Include the intended outputs so the reviewer can assess the actual sharing and identification questions.
  9. Graduate researcher: I'll keep collection and publication assumptions explicit rather than call the project automatically exempt.
  10. Research ethics adviser: Then the authorized office can give a case-specific determination through the established process.
Translated consent materials have unconfirmed review status

Fictional study preparation: translated participant information exists, but its review and approval status are unconfirmed; recruitment has not begun.

  1. Study coordinator: The translated consent materials are ready as files. Can recruitment begin with those versions?
  2. Ethics administrator: Could you confirm their review and approval status? File availability doesn't establish that they are authorized for use.
  3. Study coordinator: That status is unconfirmed. I assumed translation completion meant the materials were ready for participants.
  4. Ethics administrator: Please distinguish completed translation from the applicable review of informed-consent materials.
  5. Study coordinator: Should I compare the version identifiers with the approved study documents before contacting the review office?
  6. Ethics administrator: Yes. Provide the actual versions and ask which checks remain, rather than infer approval from similar titles.
  7. Study coordinator: I'll keep recruitment pending that confirmation and avoid promising a start date we don't yet control.
  8. Ethics administrator: Good. Also identify any proposed changes to the information content, not just the language label.
  9. Study coordinator: I'll bring the version record and open questions through the established ethics process.
  10. Ethics administrator: Then the responsible office can clarify the authorized materials without an unsupported assumption about readiness.

A pending route is not a permission

A researcher proposes a new use of coded survey data. The team can access the linking key. The institution requires a designated-office determination before new analysis. The request has been sent but no decision issued.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Researcher: "The files are coded, not automatically ___."

Answer and explanation

C. anonymous

The accessible linking key means coding does not establish anonymity to the team.

2. Coordinator: "The proposed use still awaits an authorized ___."

Answer and explanation

D. determination

Sending the request does not complete the required institutional decision.

3. Researcher: "Our team can access the linking ___."

Answer and explanation

C. key

The brief confirms that the team can link participant codes to identities.

4. Coordinator: "We can accurately report that review is ___."

Answer and explanation

C. pending

No decision has been issued, so pending is the supported status.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

REFERENCE
Course: Higher Education and Research English
Professional audience: faculty, postdoctoral researchers, graduate students, lab managers, research administrators, grant staff, ethics-board coordinators, and academic program leaders
Scope: All cases and figures are fictional. This book teaches research communication, not statistical, clinical, or legal advice or ethics approval. Follow the relevant institution, funder, jurisdiction, and journal requirements; obtain required review before conducting or changing research.
Lesson 04: Research Ethics and Human Subjects
Original fictional case: Researcher Tessa wants to reuse coded interviews from a student-stress study for a new employment-discrimination question. Her research team can access the key linking codes to participants. The original approval and consent materials have not been checked against the new purpose. The fictional institution requires its designated ethics office to determine the permitted route before new analysis or sharing begins. No determination, amendment, waiver, or new consent has been granted. Tessa can prepare the proposal and gather existing documents, but must not begin the proposed reuse or promise collaborators access.
Language workshop: Possessing the data is not permission for a new use
Communication goal: Describe a secondary-use proposal and seek an authorized ethics determination without assuming coded data is anonymous.
Language guidance: Coded does not automatically mean anonymous. Here, the team can access a linking key, so the identity connection matters. The new question and access arrangements must be described accurately to the designated reviewer.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): "The proposed reuse concerns employment discrimination." / "The interviews were collected for a student-stress study." / "Our team can access the code-to-participant key." / "Coding does not make these data anonymous to us." / "We need the original approval and consent materials." / "The new purpose has not been assessed against those documents." / "The designated ethics office determines the permitted route." / "Do not start the new analysis or sharing yet." / "An amendment may be needed; none has been approved." / "No waiver or new consent has been granted." / "We can describe the question, fields, recipients, and safeguards." / "Gathering documents is not approval to analyze the interviews." / "Access is subject to the authorized determination." / "Keep the current access restrictions in place." / "The proposal is awaiting review, not approved." / "Submit the complete proposal and wait for the required decision."
Vocabulary: secondary use: Using previously collected material for a further purpose. / human-subjects research: Research involving people or qualifying information under applicable rules. / ethics review: Examination of a research proposal's ethical acceptability and requirements. / institutional review board: A U.S. committee reviewing covered human-subjects research. / research ethics committee: A body reviewing research ethics under its remit. / informed consent: A process supporting an informed, voluntary participation decision. / consent scope: The activities and uses covered by the relevant consent. / approval scope: The work permitted by a particular authorization. / protocol amendment: A proposed change to an approved research protocol. / waiver: An authorized exception to a specified requirement. / exemption determination: An authorized decision about an applicable exemption category. / coded data: Data using codes with a separate identity-linking key. / linking key: Information connecting study codes to identities. / identifiability: The possibility of connecting information with a person. / de-identification: Removing or reducing identifying information under a specified method. / confidentiality: Protection against unauthorized information disclosure. / data-use agreement: Terms governing permitted access and use. / authorized determination: A decision made by the designated competent authority. / recontact: Contacting a participant again after earlier involvement. / participant information: Material explaining a study to potential participants. / voluntariness: Freedom to choose participation without improper pressure. / access restriction: A limit on who may obtain or use information. / purpose change: A shift from the original use to a different objective. / review status: The current stage or outcome of a review request.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.

3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.

5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

REFERENCE
Course: Higher Education and Research English
Professional audience: faculty, postdoctoral researchers, graduate students, lab managers, research administrators, grant staff, ethics-board coordinators, and academic program leaders
Scope: All cases and figures are fictional. This book teaches research communication, not statistical, clinical, or legal advice or ethics approval. Follow the relevant institution, funder, jurisdiction, and journal requirements; obtain required review before conducting or changing research.
Lesson 04: Research Ethics and Human Subjects
Original fictional case: Researcher Tessa wants to reuse coded interviews from a student-stress study for a new employment-discrimination question. Her research team can access the key linking codes to participants. The original approval and consent materials have not been checked against the new purpose. The fictional institution requires its designated ethics office to determine the permitted route before new analysis or sharing begins. No determination, amendment, waiver, or new consent has been granted. Tessa can prepare the proposal and gather existing documents, but must not begin the proposed reuse or promise collaborators access.
Language workshop: Possessing the data is not permission for a new use
Communication goal: Describe a secondary-use proposal and seek an authorized ethics determination without assuming coded data is anonymous.
Language guidance: Coded does not automatically mean anonymous. Here, the team can access a linking key, so the identity connection matters. The new question and access arrangements must be described accurately to the designated reviewer.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): "The proposed reuse concerns employment discrimination." / "The interviews were collected for a student-stress study." / "Our team can access the code-to-participant key." / "Coding does not make these data anonymous to us." / "We need the original approval and consent materials." / "The new purpose has not been assessed against those documents." / "The designated ethics office determines the permitted route." / "Do not start the new analysis or sharing yet." / "An amendment may be needed; none has been approved." / "No waiver or new consent has been granted." / "We can describe the question, fields, recipients, and safeguards." / "Gathering documents is not approval to analyze the interviews." / "Access is subject to the authorized determination." / "Keep the current access restrictions in place." / "The proposal is awaiting review, not approved." / "Submit the complete proposal and wait for the required decision."
Vocabulary: secondary use: Using previously collected material for a further purpose. / human-subjects research: Research involving people or qualifying information under applicable rules. / ethics review: Examination of a research proposal's ethical acceptability and requirements. / institutional review board: A U.S. committee reviewing covered human-subjects research. / research ethics committee: A body reviewing research ethics under its remit. / informed consent: A process supporting an informed, voluntary participation decision. / consent scope: The activities and uses covered by the relevant consent. / approval scope: The work permitted by a particular authorization. / protocol amendment: A proposed change to an approved research protocol. / waiver: An authorized exception to a specified requirement. / exemption determination: An authorized decision about an applicable exemption category. / coded data: Data using codes with a separate identity-linking key. / linking key: Information connecting study codes to identities. / identifiability: The possibility of connecting information with a person. / de-identification: Removing or reducing identifying information under a specified method. / confidentiality: Protection against unauthorized information disclosure. / data-use agreement: Terms governing permitted access and use. / authorized determination: A decision made by the designated competent authority. / recontact: Contacting a participant again after earlier involvement. / participant information: Material explaining a study to potential participants. / voluntariness: Freedom to choose participation without improper pressure. / access restriction: A limit on who may obtain or use information. / purpose change: A shift from the original use to a different objective. / review status: The current stage or outcome of a review request.
Speaking task: Read turns 3-8, emphasizing that the research team can access the linking key. Repeat turns 15-18, separating a general field description from sharing participant information. Switch roles for the transfer and read the four completed lines without adding an approval claim.
Partner: Tessa: Researcher / Omar: Research ethics coordinator
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: A researcher proposes a new use of coded survey data. The team can access the linking key. The institution requires a designated-office determination before new analysis. The request has been sent but no decision issued.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Tessa: I will gather the approval, consent materials, key-access details, and proposed analysis description. That gives the office enough context to assess the new use.
Omar: Include the proposed data-use agreement arrangements if collaboration is planned. Such terms may be relevant, but they do not replace the required ethics determination or establish participant consent by themselves.
Tessa: My update will say the proposal is being prepared, no new analysis has begun, and collaborator access remains subject to the designated review.
Omar: That accurately describes the secondary use. Keep preparation separate from conducting the new work. Once the authorized decision is recorded, follow its conditions rather than treating review as unlimited permission.
END REFERENCE
05Authorship, Collaboration, and CreditCredit follows contributions, not job titles+

Your goal

Discuss authorship eligibility, outstanding responsibilities, and author order without granting or denying credit by status.

This lesson in the book, page 40 ↗

A Read the situation

Credit follows contributions, not job titles

Research fellow Harper and project lead Marta discuss a manuscript whose target journal uses ICMJE authorship criteria: substantive research contribution, intellectual drafting or review, final-version approval, and accountability. All four are required. Harper designed and performed the main analysis but has not yet been invited to review the manuscript or approve the final version. Professor Nolan obtained funding only. The team has not agreed author order or documented contributions. Harper must have a genuine opportunity to complete the remaining responsibilities; funding alone does not establish Nolan's authorship. No final author list or order is settled.

Harper: Research fellow / Marta: Project lead

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What substantive contribution has Harper made?

Answer and explanation

C. Designed and performed the main analysis

The brief records Harper's analysis design and execution as the substantive contribution.

2. Does funding alone establish Nolan's authorship under the stated criteria?

Answer and explanation

B. No

The adopted criteria require more than obtaining funding alone.

3. What should happen before the author list is finalized?

Answer and explanation

C. Give Harper a genuine opportunity to review, approve, and accept the relevant responsibilities.

A substantive contributor should not be excluded by denying opportunities to fulfill the remaining criteria.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Authorship eligibility and author order are separate decisions. A substantive analysis contribution matters, but the supplied journal requires all four responsibilities. Give Harper the opportunity to complete them; do not invent a final approval or a guaranteed first position.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
authorship
Credited responsibility for a published scholarly work.establish authorship eligibility
contributorship
A description of what each person did.document contributorship
byline
The author names listed on a publication.finalize the byline
author order
The sequence in which authors are listed.discuss author order
substantive contribution
Significant intellectual or research work on the project.recognize a substantive contribution
conceptualization
Development of the study's ideas or research goals.describe conceptualization
methodology
Development or design of research methods.record methodology contributions
formal analysis
Application of analytical methods to study material or data.credit formal analysis
data curation
Organizing and maintaining data for use.describe data curation
investigation
Conducting the research or collecting relevant evidence.record investigation work
funding acquisition
Obtaining financial support for a project.acknowledge funding acquisition
supervision
Oversight or guidance of research work.distinguish supervision from authorship
critical review
Intellectual examination of a manuscript's content.contribute critical review
final approval
Agreement to the version intended for publication.obtain final approval
accountability
Responsibility for addressing the work's accuracy and integrity.accept accountability
corresponding author
The author managing the journal communication role.identify the corresponding author
acknowledgment
Recognition of contributions outside the author byline.agree an acknowledgment
contribution statement
A record describing each contributor's work.prepare a contribution statement
authorship criteria
The applicable conditions for being listed as an author.apply authorship criteria
honorary authorship
Listing someone without the required authorship basis.avoid honorary authorship
omitted contributor
A contributor whose relevant work is not properly recognized.address an omitted contributor
collaboration agreement
Recorded expectations for joint work.revisit the collaboration agreement
dispute resolution
A process for addressing unresolved disagreement.use the dispute-resolution process
manuscript version
An identified revision of a paper.circulate the final manuscript version

A contribution statement records work but does not automatically settle authorship. Under this case's adopted criteria, funding alone is insufficient. Apply the actual journal and institutional rules, since conventions differ by field.

C Notice the language

Discuss contribution and responsibility together

Academic hierarchy can make credit conversations difficult. Refer to the agreed publication criteria and a factual contribution record. Do not frame authorship as a personal favor, and do not exclude a substantive contributor by withholding the chance to fulfill remaining responsibilities.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Open factually
"Let us document what each collaborator contributed."
Name the analytical work
"Harper designed and performed the main analysis."
Use the agreed standard
"The target journal requires all four authorship criteria."
Identify what remains
"The manuscript review and final approval have not happened yet."
Provide a real opportunity
"Harper needs the manuscript and time to review it."
Avoid exclusion by omission
"Not receiving an invitation should not become a reason to deny credit."
Separate funding
"Funding acquisition alone does not establish authorship here."
Keep titles out of the test
"Job title is not the criterion."
Distinguish ordering
"Eligibility does not settle author order."
Avoid a premature promise
"No final position has been agreed."
Make credit visible
"Record contributions accurately in the statement."
Keep responsibility explicit
"Authorship includes accountability, not only recognition."
Identify the version
"Approval must refer to the final manuscript version."
Coordinate communication
"The corresponding-author role should be agreed."
Handle disagreement
"Use the institutional process if the group cannot resolve the dispute."
Close before submission
"Settle contributions, responsibilities, and the author list before submitting."
6 language and precision notes
Contributed
Specify the actual work rather than relying on a title.
Eligible
Must be assessed against the applicable criteria.
First author
An ordering decision, not automatic from one criterion.
Corresponding
A communication role, not necessarily the first-listed author.
Approved
Requires a real decision about an identified version.
Acknowledged
Recognition distinct from inclusion in the byline.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which statement about Harper is supported?

Answer and explanation

A. Harper has a substantive analysis contribution and outstanding authorship responsibilities.

The analysis is documented, while review, final approval, and related responsibilities remain to be completed.

2. Which action would unfairly block completion of the criteria?

Answer and explanation

D. Withholding the manuscript and then citing lack of review as a reason for exclusion

The contributor cannot fulfill review responsibilities if the group denies the opportunity to do so.

3. What does Nolan's funding-only role establish?

Answer and explanation

B. A contribution to record, but not sufficient authorship under the adopted criteria

Funding is a real contribution but does not alone meet the journal's stated authorship requirements.

4. What remains unresolved after identifying a substantive contribution?

Answer and explanation

D. Completion of the remaining responsibilities and agreement on the author list and order

Substantive contribution is only part of the process, and the brief leaves final authorship decisions unsettled.
