E Conversations

Credit follows contributions, not job titles

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Harper: Research fellow / Marta: Project lead

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • critical review
  • Accountability
  • funding acquisition
  • contributorship
  • final approval
  • contribution statement
  • dispute resolution
  • author order
  • corresponding author
  • byline
  1. Harper
    My name was missing from yesterday's draft author list. I designed and performed the main analysis, but nobody discussed credit with me.
  2. Marta
    We should start with a 1. Your analytical work must be recorded accurately. The list is not final, and an informal draft should not substitute for a proper discussion with the collaborators.
  3. Harper
    Nobody sent me the manuscript. I need to check whether the methods and interpretation describe my work correctly.
  4. Marta
    You need a genuine opportunity for 2. I will circulate the manuscript and arrange time for your response. We should not withhold that opportunity and then cite its absence against you.
  5. Harper
    The journal uses ICMJE criteria, so I understand that doing the analysis is not the only responsibility. What still needs to happen before submission?
  6. Marta
    Beyond the substantive work and intellectual review, we need 3 of the publication version and agreement on accountability. Those are real responsibilities, not boxes we can mark on someone's behalf.
  7. Harper
    I will review the full paper and discuss the analysis. Please do not claim my approval before that happens.
  8. Marta
    Agreed. 4 must be understood and accepted. We will record the status honestly and identify outstanding steps, rather than inventing agreement to meet the submission timetable.
  9. Harper
    Professor Nolan is listed already, but his contribution so far was funding, without the other responsibilities.
  10. Marta
    We should record 5 accurately too. Under the adopted criteria, that alone does not establish authorship. His title should neither replace the criteria nor prevent a respectful discussion of his actual contribution.
  11. Harper
    I also need us to discuss order. I had expected first authorship for the analysis work, but I realize we never agreed that as a group.
  12. Marta
    We must discuss 6 separately. The criteria establish eligibility, not a universal ordering formula. We need a transparent conversation about the project and the journal's conventions, without promising a position prematurely.
  13. Harper
    Please invite the data collection and curation leads too. I do not want us settling my position without hearing what everyone else contributed.
  14. Marta
    Yes. Accurate 7 requires the whole project's contribution record. We should not decide credit solely through the loudest objection, personal seniority, or who happened to see the draft first.
  15. Harper
    Who will communicate with the journal? People sometimes describe that role as though it always means first author.
  16. Marta
    The 8 role should be agreed explicitly. It concerns communication and associated submission responsibilities. It does not automatically determine first position, and it cannot resolve an unsettled authorship dispute by itself.
  17. Harper
    If the group still cannot agree after reviewing contributions and responsibilities, we need a fair route forward rather than submitting an unresolved list.
  18. Marta
    Use the institutional 9 process if necessary. We should document the issue and seek the appropriate assistance. A deadline does not justify bypassing a substantive disagreement about credit.
  19. Harper
    For now, please circulate the manuscript, record the analysis contribution, and arrange a group discussion. I will review the content before giving any final approval.
  20. Marta
    Then we can finalize the 10 on an accurate basis before submission. No final list or order is settled today; the next step is to complete the review and resolve the outstanding decisions.

Answers and explanations
  1. contribution statement Turn 2. A contribution statement records the actual work performed, providing a factual basis for the credit discussion.
  2. critical review Turn 4. Critical review is an intellectual examination of the manuscript, which Harper should be able to undertake rather than being excluded for not receiving it.
  3. final approval Turn 6. Final approval concerns the identified publication version and cannot be assumed before Harper has seen and agreed to it.
  4. Accountability Turn 8. Accountability connects authorship with responsibility for addressing accuracy and integrity questions, not merely receiving credit.
  5. funding acquisition Turn 10. Funding acquisition is a contribution to recognize, but by itself does not meet the adopted journal's authorship criteria.
  6. author order Turn 12. Author order is a separate collective decision and is not automatically determined by identifying one substantive contribution.
  7. contributorship Turn 14. Contributorship describes each person's work and helps the group avoid overlooking relevant contributions while assessing the author list.
  8. corresponding author Turn 16. The corresponding author handles journal communications, a role distinct from a guaranteed first position in the byline.
  9. dispute resolution Turn 18. Dispute resolution provides a process for unresolved authorship disagreement rather than treating submission or hierarchy as a substitute for agreement.
  10. byline Turn 20. The byline is the eventual list of authors, which should reflect resolved eligibility and agreed responsibilities rather than the current informal draft.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Harper: My name was missing from yesterday's draft author list. I designed and performed the main analysis, but nobody discussed credit with me.
  2. Marta: We should start with a contribution statement. Your analytical work must be recorded accurately. The list is not final, and an informal draft should not substitute for a proper discussion with the collaborators.
  3. Harper: Nobody sent me the manuscript. I need to check whether the methods and interpretation describe my work correctly.
  4. Marta: You need a genuine opportunity for critical review. I will circulate the manuscript and arrange time for your response. We should not withhold that opportunity and then cite its absence against you.
  5. Harper: The journal uses ICMJE criteria, so I understand that doing the analysis is not the only responsibility. What still needs to happen before submission?
  6. Marta: Beyond the substantive work and intellectual review, we need final approval of the publication version and agreement on accountability. Those are real responsibilities, not boxes we can mark on someone's behalf.
  7. Harper: I will review the full paper and discuss the analysis. Please do not claim my approval before that happens.
  8. Marta: Agreed. Accountability must be understood and accepted. We will record the status honestly and identify outstanding steps, rather than inventing agreement to meet the submission timetable.
  9. Harper: Professor Nolan is listed already, but his contribution so far was funding, without the other responsibilities.
  10. Marta: We should record funding acquisition accurately too. Under the adopted criteria, that alone does not establish authorship. His title should neither replace the criteria nor prevent a respectful discussion of his actual contribution.
  11. Harper: I also need us to discuss order. I had expected first authorship for the analysis work, but I realize we never agreed that as a group.
  12. Marta: We must discuss author order separately. The criteria establish eligibility, not a universal ordering formula. We need a transparent conversation about the project and the journal's conventions, without promising a position prematurely.
  13. Harper: Please invite the data collection and curation leads too. I do not want us settling my position without hearing what everyone else contributed.
  14. Marta: Yes. Accurate contributorship requires the whole project's contribution record. We should not decide credit solely through the loudest objection, personal seniority, or who happened to see the draft first.
  15. Harper: Who will communicate with the journal? People sometimes describe that role as though it always means first author.
  16. Marta: The corresponding author role should be agreed explicitly. It concerns communication and associated submission responsibilities. It does not automatically determine first position, and it cannot resolve an unsettled authorship dispute by itself.

Dialogue in the book, page 46 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Discuss contributions before deciding authorship

Separate fictional credit discussion: a collaborating researcher designed an interview guide and analyzed 20 transcripts, but has not drafted manuscript text. The researcher expected authorship; a draft lists an acknowledgment. Applicable criteria have not been reviewed.

  1. Project coordinator: The draft lists an acknowledgment. Could you explain your contribution and what credit you expected?
  2. Collaborating researcher: I designed the interview guide and analyzed twenty transcripts. I expected authorship, although I haven't drafted manuscript text.
  3. Project coordinator: So your contribution includes design and analysis, but not manuscript drafting. Have I captured that accurately?
  4. Collaborating researcher: Yes. Please distinguish analysis from data collection; those were different contributions.
  5. Project coordinator: Agreed. I'll record analysis separately. What concern do you want the group to address first?
  6. Collaborating researcher: Whether my contribution meets the applicable authorship criteria, rather than whether my job title sounds senior enough.
  7. Project coordinator: Authorship remains undecided. May I review the journal criteria and institutional guidance before we discuss eligibility together?
  8. Collaborating researcher: Yes. Please bring the criteria and include my account in that discussion, not just the acknowledgment proposal.
  9. Project coordinator: I'll own that review and circulate the unresolved eligibility question; today's summary doesn't approve either credit category.
  10. Collaborating researcher: Confirmed. The contribution summary is accurate, and I understand that authorship is still under review.
A software contributor was left out of the discussion

Separate fictional collaboration: a research software engineer built the analysis pipeline and tested missing-value handling but was absent from the credit discussion. The engineer expects authorship consideration and an accurate software contribution description. Eligibility is unassessed.

  1. Corresponding author: You weren't included in the credit discussion. What contribution and expectation did we miss?
  2. Research software engineer: I built the analysis pipeline and tested missing-value handling. I expected authorship consideration, not an automatic decision.
  3. Corresponding author: The draft calls it technical assistance. Does that describe your work accurately enough?
  4. Research software engineer: No. It hides the pipeline development and testing. Please name those activities when we assess my contribution.
  5. Corresponding author: Then the summary should say software development and missing-value testing, with authorship consideration requested. Is that accurate?
  6. Research software engineer: Yes. I also want to understand the applicable criteria before anyone treats software work as automatically excluded.
  7. Corresponding author: I'll review those criteria and the institution's advice route; corresponding-author coordination doesn't settle eligibility by itself.
  8. Research software engineer: Thank you. Can we keep acknowledgment and authorship distinct until that review and group discussion?
  9. Corresponding author: Agreed. I'll own the criteria review and bring your confirmed contribution summary; no credit decision is final.
  10. Research software engineer: That addresses my concern about being omitted while leaving the substantive authorship question open.
An old author order is not current agreement

Separate fictional manuscript discussion: an early draft lists a collaborating statistician third. Since that draft, the statistician completed a sensitivity analysis and revised the results interpretation. The statistician requests an order review, not a guaranteed position. Current agreement and applicable ordering guidance are unconfirmed.

  1. Manuscript coordinator: The early draft lists you third. What changed in your contribution or expectations since then?
  2. Collaborating statistician: I completed the sensitivity analysis and revised the results interpretation. I want the order reviewed, not a guaranteed position.
  3. Manuscript coordinator: So you're asking us to consider those later contributions rather than automatically move you first?
  4. Collaborating statistician: Correct. Without everyone's updated contributions, I can't justify a specific order.
  5. Manuscript coordinator: I'll summarize your added analysis and interpretation work, with a request for collective review. Does that capture your position?
  6. Collaborating statistician: Yes. Please don't treat my earlier silence as agreement with the current author list.
  7. Manuscript coordinator: Understood. I'll review applicable ordering guidance and ask every collaborator to confirm their current contribution record.
  8. Collaborating statistician: Who will coordinate unresolved criteria questions before the list is represented as agreed for submission?
  9. Manuscript coordinator: I'll own that review and coordinate the appropriate advice request. Today's exchange confirms your account, not authorship or order.
  10. Collaborating statistician: Agreed. Keep the list provisional until the relevant criteria and collaborators' perspectives have been reviewed.

An invitation is not an approval

A journal uses the same four criteria. A collaborator made a substantive methods contribution and has just received the manuscript for review. No final approval or accountability agreement is yet recorded, and author order is undecided.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Lead: "The methods work is a substantive ___."

Answer and explanation

B. contribution

The supplied methods work satisfies the stated substantive-contribution element.

2. Collaborator: "Receiving the manuscript gives me an opportunity for ___."

Answer and explanation

C. critical review

Circulation enables review but does not mean that intellectual review has already occurred.

3. Lead: "Final approval remains ___."

Answer and explanation

A. outstanding

The brief states that no final approval has yet been recorded.

4. Collaborator: "Eligibility alone does not settle ___."

Answer and explanation

D. author order

The ordering decision remains separate and explicitly unresolved in this case.

Vocabulary: authorship: Credited responsibility for a published scholarly work. / contributorship: A description of what each person did. / byline: The author names listed on a publication. / author order: The sequence in which authors are listed. / substantive contribution: Significant intellectual or research work on the project. / conceptualization: Development of the study's ideas or research goals. / methodology: Development or design of research methods. / formal analysis: Application of analytical methods to study material or data. / data curation: Organizing and maintaining data for use. / investigation: Conducting the research or collecting relevant evidence. / funding acquisition: Obtaining financial support for a project. / supervision: Oversight or guidance of research work. / critical review: Intellectual examination of a manuscript's content. / final approval: Agreement to the version intended for publication. / accountability: Responsibility for addressing the work's accuracy and integrity. / corresponding author: The author managing the journal communication role. / acknowledgment: Recognition of contributions outside the author byline. / contribution statement: A record describing each contributor's work. / authorship criteria: The applicable conditions for being listed as an author. / honorary authorship: Listing someone without the required authorship basis. / omitted contributor: A contributor whose relevant work is not properly recognized. / collaboration agreement: Recorded expectations for joint work. / dispute resolution: A process for addressing unresolved disagreement. / manuscript version: An identified revision of a paper.

Practice grammar in context — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

06Peer Review and Revision ResponsesRevise the claim, not the observed result+

Your goal

Respond to a valid reviewer criticism with a precise revision and traceable manuscript locations.

This lesson in the book, page 49 ↗

A Read the situation

Revise the claim, not the observed result

Authors Mei and Luca respond to a reviewer of a fictional study involving 60 volunteer students at one campus. The study measured self-reported confidence after one workshop, with a mean of 7.1 on a ten-point scale. There was no baseline measurement, comparison group, or direct learning test. The manuscript nevertheless says the workshop improves learning for all adults. The authors agree to remove that claim from the abstract and conclusion, report the observed confidence in the studied sample, and explain the design limits. No new data or reanalysis has been performed.

Mei: Corresponding author / Luca: Coauthor

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What did the study directly measure?

Answer and explanation

C. Self-reported confidence after one workshop

The measured outcome was post-workshop confidence, not demonstrated learning or change.

2. Which original claim goes beyond the evidence?

Answer and explanation

C. The workshop improves learning for all adults.

The original claim substitutes learning for confidence and extends beyond the study population and design.

3. What has been done for this revision?

Answer and explanation

D. Wording is being corrected; no new data or reanalysis has occurred.

The brief permits a reporting correction and explicitly states no new data or reanalysis.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

A post-workshop confidence mean describes a measured state, not improvement from baseline. Confidence and demonstrated learning are also different constructs. Changing the claim can correct reporting without altering the observed 7.1 mean.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
peer review
Expert evaluation of scholarly work before or after publication.respond to peer review
reviewer comment
A point raised by a manuscript evaluator.address a reviewer comment
response letter
The document explaining replies and revisions.prepare a response letter
point-by-point response
A reply organized against each review issue.provide a point-by-point response
revision
A change to a manuscript or associated material.document the revision
tracked changes
Visible marking of edits between document versions.supply tracked changes
revised manuscript
The updated version submitted for consideration.check the revised manuscript
abstract
A concise summary of a scholarly work.revise the abstract
conclusion
The manuscript's final interpretation or takeaway.narrow the conclusion
claim
An assertion about what the evidence establishes.qualify a claim
overgeneralization
Extending a conclusion beyond its support.remove an overgeneralization
sampling frame
The operational list or source of units available for selection, which may not fully cover the target population.describe the sampling frame
volunteer sample
Participants who chose to take part.acknowledge the volunteer sample
single-site study
Research conducted at one location.state the single-site limitation
self-report
Information supplied by participants about themselves.distinguish self-report from direct measurement
post-only measure
An outcome recorded after an event without a baseline measure.interpret a post-only measure
baseline measure
An observation collected before the relevant intervention or period.identify the missing baseline measure
comparison condition
A reference condition for evaluating a contrast.specify a comparison condition
construct
The concept a measure is intended to represent.identify the measured construct
learning assessment
A task directly assessing relevant learning.distinguish a learning assessment
limitation
A feature restricting interpretation or application.state the study limitation
reanalysis
A new analysis of existing data.distinguish revision from reanalysis
page-and-line reference
A precise locator for manuscript text.provide page-and-line references
editorial decision
A journal editor's determination about the manuscript.await the editorial decision

The volunteer, single-campus sample limits broad application. An honest limitation statement does not repair the design or guarantee acceptance. Cite locations in the actual revised version, not stale page and line numbers from an earlier draft.

C Notice the language

Agreement can be specific and professional

A reviewer response need not defend every original sentence. Acknowledge the valid point, say exactly what changed, and identify where it changed. Do not claim a limitation has been solved merely because the revised text now describes it honestly.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Acknowledge the point
"We agree that the original claim exceeded the design."
Identify the measured construct
"We measured self-reported confidence, not demonstrated learning."
State the population
"The sample comprised 60 volunteers at one campus."
Keep the observation
"The observed mean remains 7.1 out of ten."
Remove an unsupported change
"Without a baseline, we cannot claim improvement."
Remove a causal claim
"There was no comparison group to support that inference."
Bound generalization
"The result should not be stated for all adults."
Describe the revision
"We have narrowed the abstract and conclusion."
Make the location traceable
"The response identifies the revised page and lines."
Avoid inventing work
"No new data collection or reanalysis was performed."
Separate honesty from repair
"The limitation is now explicit, not eliminated."
Answer the comment
"The revised wording reports the observation in the studied sample."
Check consistency
"The same claim boundary appears throughout the manuscript."
Use a clean response
"We state the change and its reason directly."
Respect the decision
"The editor will assess the revised submission."
Close the issue
"The response and manuscript now describe the same evidence."
6 language and precision notes
Improves
A change or effect claim requiring appropriate evidence.
Confidence
Not interchangeable with measured learning.
Volunteers
A sample feature relevant to interpretation.
Revised
Does not mean new data were collected.
Addressed
Can mean clarified, not that a design limitation disappeared.
Accepted
An editorial status, not guaranteed by agreement with a reviewer.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which revised claim is supported?

Answer and explanation

A. Sixty volunteer students at one campus reported mean post-workshop confidence of 7.1 out of ten.

The supported statement reports the measured outcome and sample without inventing a baseline or causal design.

2. Which response accurately describes the work?

Answer and explanation

A. The abstract and conclusion are narrowed; no reanalysis was performed.

The revision changes interpretation and reporting, not the dataset or analysis.

3. What does acknowledging the limitation accomplish?

Answer and explanation

B. Makes the interpretation more accurate without removing the design constraint

Explicit reporting improves transparency but cannot alter how the completed study was designed.

4. Which locator should the response use?

Answer and explanation

C. Page and line numbers verified against the revised manuscript

Accurate locations let the reviewer find the actual correction in the submitted revision.

Check explanations in the book, page 95 ↗

E Conversations

Revise the claim, not the observed result

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Mei: Corresponding author / Luca: Coauthor

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • limitation
  • page-and-line reference
  • point-by-point response
  • editorial decision
  • revision
  • construct
  • revised manuscript
  • reanalysis
  • overgeneralization
  • post-only measure
  1. Mei
    The reviewer says our claim about improving learning for all adults exceeds the sample and design. I agree, but the response needs to explain the correction precisely.
  2. Luca
    Start with the measured 1. We asked sixty volunteer students at one campus about confidence after the workshop. We did not directly assess learning, and the distinction belongs in the revised wording.
  3. Mei
    The mean was seven point one on a ten-point scale. That observed number can remain, provided we do not call it a seven-point improvement.
  4. Luca
    Right. It is a 2. We have no baseline measurement, so we cannot calculate a before-and-after change. The original sentence implied a comparison we never measured.
  5. Mei
    We also had no comparison group. Narrowing the claim should therefore address both the measured outcome and the lack of a design supporting that causal language.
  6. Luca
    State the 3 directly. The study reports post-workshop confidence in this sample. It does not isolate the workshop's effect, and revising the wording does not change that design boundary.
  7. Mei
    What about all adults? Even if we had measured learning, sixty volunteers at one campus would not establish the same result in every adult population.
  8. Luca
    Remove that 4. The revised statement should name the observed sample. We can discuss broader questions as future research without presenting them as conclusions already established by these participants.
  9. Mei
    The abstract and conclusion both contain the overclaim. We need to change both, not just the quoted sentence.
  10. Luca
    Check the 5 throughout. The title, summary, results description, and final interpretation should not quietly restore the same unsupported claim after we have corrected it elsewhere.
  11. Mei
    I will quote the replacement sentence in our reply, then give its location. Could you check that the conclusion now says the same thing?
  12. Luca
    Use a 6. For this comment, identify the outcome correction, the population boundary, and the design limitation. The reviewer should be able to verify each without guessing what we meant.
  13. Mei
    I will add the page and line numbers after the revised file is finalized. The current numbers could shift as we edit the abstract.
  14. Luca
    A checked 7 matters. Use the submitted revision's locations, not an earlier draft's numbering. We should also keep the response wording consistent with the actual replacement text.
  15. Mei
    Someone suggested claiming additional analyses. We have not done them, and an impressive-sounding statement would not solve the problem.
  16. Luca
    Do not claim 8. No new data or analysis was added. This revision corrects the interpretation and reporting of the existing observation; it does not create evidence the study never collected.
  17. Mei
    I have written limitation addressed. That sounds as though we fixed the study design. Would limitation clarified describe what we actually did?
  18. Luca
    Say the 9 makes the boundary explicit. That is accurate and useful. We have improved the manuscript's claims, not repaired the completed design or established an effect through wording alone.
  19. Mei
    I will keep the mean, remove the improvement and all-adults claims, and verify locations before resubmission.
  20. Luca
    Then await the 10. A clear correction supports evaluation, but it does not guarantee acceptance. Our responsibility is to make the evidence, changes, and remaining limitations straightforward to inspect.

Answers and explanations
  1. construct Turn 2. The measured construct was self-reported confidence, not demonstrated learning, so the original claim described a different outcome.
  2. post-only measure Turn 4. A post-only measure records the state after the workshop without a baseline from which to calculate improvement.
  3. limitation Turn 6. The limitation restricts interpretation; making it explicit does not retroactively add a baseline or comparison group.
  4. overgeneralization Turn 8. Overgeneralization extends the conclusion beyond the volunteer, single-campus sample without supporting evidence.
  5. revised manuscript Turn 10. The revised manuscript must use consistent claim boundaries across sections, rather than correcting only one quoted sentence.
  6. point-by-point response Turn 12. A point-by-point response links the specific reviewer concern to the corresponding explanation and revision.
  7. page-and-line reference Turn 14. A page-and-line reference must match the revised version so the reviewer can locate the actual correction.
  8. reanalysis Turn 16. Reanalysis means new analysis of existing data, which the brief explicitly says has not occurred.
  9. revision Turn 18. The revision clarifies the interpretation and limitation rather than eliminating the underlying design constraint.
  10. editorial decision Turn 20. The editorial decision remains with the journal; a valid correction does not guarantee acceptance.
Read the complete conversation

  17. Mei: I have written limitation addressed. That sounds as though we fixed the study design. Would limitation clarified describe what we actually did?
  18. Luca: Say the revision makes the boundary explicit. That is accurate and useful. We have improved the manuscript's claims, not repaired the completed design or established an effect through wording alone.
  19. Mei: I will keep the mean, remove the improvement and all-adults claims, and verify locations before resubmission.
  20. Luca: Then await the editorial decision. A clear correction supports evaluation, but it does not guarantee acceptance. Our responsibility is to make the evidence, changes, and remaining limitations straightforward to inspect.
Dialogue in the book, page 55 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

A claim extends beyond the study sample

Fictional peer-review discussion using the lesson case: a reviewer identifies an overgeneralized claim, and the authors agree the wording needs revision.

  1. Corresponding author: The reviewer says our claim goes beyond the sample. Do you agree we need to correct it?
  2. Coauthor: Yes. We described a general population although the study only supports a narrower sample-based statement.
  3. Corresponding author: How should we acknowledge that in the response letter without pretending the underlying data changed?
  4. Coauthor: We agree the original wording exceeded the study's scope and have narrowed the claim to the observed sample.
  5. Corresponding author: That is the response we can use after the revision is actually made, not before.
  6. Coauthor: Correct. For now, the revision is planned; we should confirm the text and locations before claiming completion.
  7. Corresponding author: Could you give a model replacement that preserves the result but removes the universal wording?
  8. Coauthor: In the sampled group, we observed the reported pattern; broader applicability remains untested.
  9. Corresponding author: I'll revise the manuscript and check the response against the actual changes during our major-revision review.
  10. Coauthor: Good. The correction should acknowledge the overstatement without claiming new evidence or dismissing the reviewer.
The response says added, but the analysis is only proposed

Fictional revision meeting: a draft response says an additional analysis was added, although the team has only proposed it and has not run it.

  1. Revision coordinator: The response letter says we added an analysis, but it hasn't been run. We need to correct that.
  2. Coauthor: You're right. I wrote the planned response as though the work were already complete.
  3. Revision coordinator: Please give an accurate interim statement for our internal review, keeping the journal response unsubmitted.
  4. Coauthor: We propose an additional analysis to address this comment; its feasibility and results are not yet established.
  5. Revision coordinator: That separates intention from completion. We also shouldn't predict that the analysis will confirm our original claim.
  6. Coauthor: Agreed. Its outcome is unknown, and any final response must reflect what we actually do and find.
  7. Revision coordinator: If the analysis proves infeasible, we should explain the limitation rather than fabricate an addition.
  8. Coauthor: I'll discuss scope with the analysis owner and replace the premature completion wording now.
  9. Revision coordinator: Before submission, let's verify every change claim against the revised manuscript and available results.
  10. Coauthor: Yes. The final peer-review response will describe completed work accurately and identify any reasoned limitation.
Clarify an ambiguous comparison in the manuscript

Fictional peer review: a reviewer read better outcomes as a between-group comparison, but the analysis only describes change within one group.

  1. Lead author: The reviewer interpreted better outcomes as a comparison between groups. Our analysis only examines one group's change.
  2. Coauthor: Our wording invited that interpretation. We should acknowledge the ambiguity rather than blame the reviewer.
  3. Lead author: What replacement makes the analytical scope clear without claiming a causal effect?
  4. Coauthor: The observed outcome changed within the sampled group over the study period; no between-group effect was estimated.
  5. Lead author: That states what we measured and what we didn't. Should we also revisit the abstract?
  6. Coauthor: Yes. The same ambiguous phrase may appear elsewhere, but we should check rather than assume it does.
  7. Lead author: I'll review the claim locations and align the response letter with the actual revisions.
  8. Coauthor: Please distinguish the wording correction from any new analysis; we aren't adding a comparison dataset here.
  9. Lead author: The response will explain that we clarified the scope and retain the relevant limitation.
  10. Coauthor: Good. That directly addresses the reader's concern without overstating either the original analysis or the revision.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

Revise the claim, not the observed result
  1. Read turns 1-8, distinguishing a post-only confidence score from improvement in learning.
  2. Repeat turns 11-18, making the revision location and lack of reanalysis explicit.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

A corrected claim, unchanged observations

A single-campus survey records post-session satisfaction from 45 volunteers, with no baseline or comparison group. Authors remove a claim of improved performance. They change wording only and collect no new data.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Author: "The measured outcome was ___."

Answer and explanation

B. satisfaction

The survey asks about satisfaction, not directly observed performance.

2. Reviewer: "The study cannot calculate improvement without a ___."

Answer and explanation

A. baseline

A post-only observation lacks the starting measure needed for a before-and-after change.

3. Author: "The work completed here is a wording ___."

Answer and explanation

D. revision

The authors changed the claim without collecting data or performing a new analysis.

4. Reviewer: "The design limitation is explicit, not ___."

Answer and explanation

D. eliminated

More accurate wording does not change the completed study's design.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Lesson 06: Peer Review and Revision Responses
Original fictional case: Authors Mei and Luca respond to a reviewer of a fictional study involving 60 volunteer students at one campus. The study measured self-reported confidence after one workshop, with a mean of 7.1 on a ten-point scale. There was no baseline measurement, comparison group, or direct learning test. The manuscript nevertheless says the workshop improves learning for all adults. The authors agree to remove that claim from the abstract and conclusion, report the observed confidence in the studied sample, and explain the design limits. No new data or reanalysis has been performed.
Language workshop: Revise the claim, not the observed result
Communication goal: Respond to a valid reviewer criticism with a precise revision and traceable manuscript locations.
Language guidance: A post-workshop confidence mean describes a measured state, not improvement from baseline. Confidence and demonstrated learning are also different constructs. Changing the claim can correct reporting without altering the observed 7.1 mean.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): "We agree that the original claim exceeded the design." / "We measured self-reported confidence, not demonstrated learning." / "The sample comprised 60 volunteers at one campus." / "The observed mean remains 7.1 out of ten." / "Without a baseline, we cannot claim improvement." / "There was no comparison group to support that inference." / "The result should not be stated for all adults." / "We have narrowed the abstract and conclusion." / "The response identifies the revised page and lines." / "No new data collection or reanalysis was performed." / "The limitation is now explicit, not eliminated." / "The revised wording reports the observation in the studied sample." / "The same claim boundary appears throughout the manuscript." / "We state the change and its reason directly." / "The editor will assess the revised submission." / "The response and manuscript now describe the same evidence."
Vocabulary: peer review: Expert evaluation of scholarly work before or after publication. / reviewer comment: A point raised by a manuscript evaluator. / response letter: The document explaining replies and revisions. / point-by-point response: A reply organized against each review issue. / revision: A change to a manuscript or associated material. / tracked changes: Visible marking of edits between document versions. / revised manuscript: The updated version submitted for consideration. / abstract: A concise summary of a scholarly work. / conclusion: The manuscript's final interpretation or takeaway. / claim: An assertion about what the evidence establishes. / overgeneralization: Extending a conclusion beyond its support. / sampling frame: The operational list or source of units available for selection, which may not fully cover the target population. / volunteer sample: Participants who chose to take part. / single-site study: Research conducted at one location. / self-report: Information supplied by participants about themselves. / post-only measure: An outcome recorded after an event without a baseline measure. / baseline measure: An observation collected before the relevant intervention or period. / comparison condition: A reference condition for evaluating a contrast. / construct: The concept a measure is intended to represent. / learning assessment: A task directly assessing relevant learning. / limitation: A feature restricting interpretation or application. / reanalysis: A new analysis of existing data. / page-and-line reference: A precise locator for manuscript text. / editorial decision: A journal editor's determination about the manuscript.
Speaking task: Read turns 1-8, distinguishing a post-only confidence score from improvement in learning. Repeat turns 11-18, making the revision location and lack of reanalysis explicit. Switch roles for the transfer and read explicit, not eliminated with a clear contrast.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

07Data Management and ReproducibilityThe missing-value code is not a temperature+

Your goal

Make a dataset interpretable through verified units, missing-value rules, dates, and traceable processing.

This lesson in the book, page 58 ↗

A Read the situation

The missing-value code is not a temperature

Research data steward Elena helps analyst Ravi prepare a small environmental dataset. The column t contains 20, 22, -999, 24, and 26. The collector confirms that t is air temperature in degrees Celsius and -999 means a failed reading, not a temperature or zero. The date label 04/05/26 is confirmed as May 4, 2026; readings were taken at 09:00 UTC. The raw file must remain unchanged. An analysis copy will mark the failed reading as missing and calculate the mean of the four valid values. The release needs a data dictionary, processing record, and identified software version.

Elena: Research data steward / Ravi: Analyst

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What does -999 represent in this dataset?

Answer and explanation

B. A failed reading coded as missing

The collector explicitly confirms that minus nine hundred ninety-nine is the failed-reading code.

2. What is the mean of the four valid temperatures?

Answer and explanation

B. 23 degrees Celsius

The valid values sum to ninety-two, and ninety-two divided by four equals twenty-three.

3. How should 04/05/26 be represented unambiguously after confirmation?

Answer and explanation

C. 2026-05-04

The collector confirms May fourth, twenty twenty-six, which matches the year-month-day representation.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

The valid values are 20, 22, 24, and 26: their mean is 23 degrees Celsius. Treating -999 as a temperature would produce -181.4. Replacing it with zero would produce 18.4. Neither represents the stated valid-observation calculation.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
data dictionary
Definitions and coding rules for dataset variables.publish a data dictionary
metadata
Information describing data, collection, or processing.supply essential metadata
variable name
The label assigned to a data field.clarify variable names
unit of measurement
The scale used to express a quantity.specify units of measurement
missing-value code
A designated value representing absent information.document missing-value codes
sentinel value
A special value used to mark a status rather than an observation.identify a sentinel value
failed reading
A measurement attempt without a valid recorded result.mark a failed reading
valid observation
A recorded value meeting the applicable inclusion rules.count valid observations
raw file
The original dataset before later cleaning or transformation.preserve the raw file
analysis copy
A derived dataset used for analysis.create a documented analysis copy
transformation log
A record of changes from source to derived data.maintain a transformation log
data cleaning
Applying documented rules to correct or prepare data.document data cleaning
imputation
Replacing missing values using a specified estimation method.distinguish cleaning from imputation
complete-case calculation
A calculation using records with required observed values.describe a complete-case calculation
date format
The convention used to represent a calendar date.standardize the date format
time zone
The reference used to interpret recorded times.record the time zone
collection timestamp
The recorded date and time of observation.preserve collection timestamps
version identifier
A label identifying a particular dataset or software revision.record the version identifier
software environment
The tools and versions used to run an analysis.document the software environment
dependency
A required library or component for a process.list analysis dependencies
readme
An introductory file explaining a dataset or project.include a readme
repository
A managed location for preserving and accessing materials.select a suitable repository
computational reproducibility
Reobtaining results with the specified data and computation.check computational reproducibility
data validation
Checks that data meet specified rules or expectations.perform data validation

The analysis uses four observed values; it does not estimate the failed reading. Preserve the raw sentinel and document its conversion to missing in the analysis copy. The collector, not a guess about regional date conventions, resolves the date.

C Notice the language

Make the file usable by someone outside the team

A familiar variable name can hide assumptions that a new collaborator cannot recover. Confirm definitions with the collector instead of guessing from plausible values. Document the transformation so a later reader can distinguish an observed value from a missing code and reproduce the analysis.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Ask for meaning
"What does the variable t represent?"
Confirm the unit
"The collector confirms degrees Celsius."
Identify the special code
"Minus 999 marks a failed reading."
Reject a false zero
"Missing is not the same as zero."
Keep the original
"The raw file remains unchanged."
Describe the derived copy
"The analysis copy marks that code as missing."
State the calculation base
"The mean uses four valid observations."
Give the result with units
"The valid-observation mean is 23 degrees Celsius."
Avoid invented data
"We have not imputed the failed reading."
Resolve the date
"The collector confirms May 4, 2026."
Use an unambiguous format
"Record the date as 2026-05-04."
Keep time meaningful
"The collection time is 09:00 UTC."
Document the fields
"The data dictionary must include units and missing codes."
Record the transformation
"Log the conversion and the calculation rule."
Identify the environment
"State the software version used for the analysis."
Check the release
"Another analyst should be able to reproduce the reported mean."
6 language and precision notes
Plausible
Not a substitute for a verified variable definition.
Missing
An absent valid observation, not zero.
Excluded from this mean
Does not mean erased from the raw record.
Imputed
An estimated replacement; none is supplied in this case.
May 4
Confirmed meaning of the otherwise ambiguous source label.
Reproducible
Computation can repeat; that alone does not prove scientific validity.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which data-handling description is correct?

Answer and explanation

D. Preserve the raw file and convert the sentinel to missing in a documented analysis copy.

Preserving the raw file retains provenance. The documented analysis copy applies the collector's confirmed missing-value rule without inventing a measurement.

2. What does using four valid observations imply?

Answer and explanation

D. No value has been imputed for the failed reading.

The calculation uses observed values only and does not estimate the failed measurement.

3. Why is the collector's date confirmation necessary?

Answer and explanation

A. The original numeric label could otherwise be interpreted in different date orders.

The source label is ambiguous without verified knowledge of its intended date convention.

4. Which release package best supports reproducibility?

Answer and explanation

B. Data, dictionary, processing steps, and the identified software environment

Interpretable data and traceable computation allow another analyst to understand and rerun the result.

Check explanations in the book, page 97 ↗

E Conversations

The missing-value code is not a temperature

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Elena: Research data steward / Ravi: Analyst

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • software environment
  • transformation log
  • missing-value code
  • time zone
  • date format
  • imputation
  • valid observations
  • raw file
  • unit of measurement
  • computational reproducibility
  1. Ravi
    The shared file has a column called t and five values. Four look like ordinary temperatures, but the fifth is minus nine hundred ninety-nine.
  2. Elena
    Do not guess the 1 from the values. The collector confirms air temperature in degrees Celsius. We need that definition in the documentation, because the column name alone does not establish it.
  3. Ravi
    The collector confirmed that minus nine hundred ninety-nine codes a failed reading, not a cold observation.
  4. Elena
    Document the 2 and mark it missing in the analysis copy. Do not use zero: that would turn a failed reading into an invented temperature.
  5. Ravi
    Including the code gives a mean of minus one hundred eighty-one point four, which is not the supported calculation.
  6. Elena
    Use the four 3 for this mean: twenty, twenty-two, twenty-four, and twenty-six. They sum to ninety-two, so the mean is twenty-three degrees Celsius. State the four-observation base alongside it.
  7. Ravi
    Replacing the code with zero would give eighteen point four instead. That is also wrong for this requested mean, even though it looks less obviously unusual.
  8. Elena
    Correct. We are not performing 4. We have no estimated replacement for the failed reading. The analysis should say exactly which observed values were used, without turning an absent measurement into a convenient number.
  9. Ravi
    I can make a separate cleaned file. Should the original keep minus nine hundred ninety-nine so the transformation can be checked later?
  10. Elena
    Preserve the 5. Keep the original code and create a documented analysis copy. Future users need both the source record and the explanation of how the derived data were produced.
  11. Ravi
    There is also a date labeled zero-four slash zero-five slash twenty-six. The collector says it means May fourth, twenty twenty-six, not April fifth.
  12. Elena
    Use an unambiguous 6 such as 2026-05-04 in the documented copy. Record how you resolved the source label. A plausible regional assumption would not be enough without that confirmation.
  13. Ravi
    The readings were taken at nine in the morning UTC. I will include that reference, because nine o'clock alone could mean different local times.
  14. Elena
    Yes, the 7 belongs with the collection information. Keep the timestamp meaning explicit so another researcher does not align these readings with observations from a different hour.
  15. Ravi
    The data dictionary will define t, the Celsius unit, the failed-reading code, and the confirmed date and time conventions. What else belongs in the release?
  16. Elena
    Add a 8 describing the missing-code conversion and mean calculation. It should connect the raw file to the analysis copy and reported result without requiring a colleague to reconstruct undocumented choices.
  17. Ravi
    I will add the software version and dependencies to the readme. Could you check that the instructions start from the raw file?
  18. Elena
    Document the 9 with the data and instructions. Even a simple calculation benefits from a traceable version, especially when this small example becomes part of a larger processing workflow.
  19. Ravi
    I will ask another analyst to follow the release instructions and reproduce the twenty-three-degree mean from the preserved source and documented rules.
  20. Elena
    That checks 10. A matching result is useful, but it does not prove scientific validity. Keep the variable definitions with the calculation so readers can check both.

Answers and explanations
  1. unit of measurement Turn 2. The unit of measurement must come from verified collection information; the collector confirms degrees Celsius in this case.
  2. missing-value code Turn 4. The missing-value code represents an absent valid reading, not an observed temperature or a zero measurement.
  3. valid observations Turn 6. The valid observations are twenty, twenty-two, twenty-four, and twenty-six, excluding the failed-reading code from the stated mean.
  4. imputation Turn 8. Imputation would estimate a replacement value, whereas this case calculates a mean from four observed temperatures without filling the missing reading.
  5. raw file Turn 10. The raw file retains the original record, including its sentinel, while documented transformations belong in a separate derived copy.
  6. date format Turn 12. The date format should reflect the confirmed May fourth date, written as 2026-05-04 rather than guessed from regional convention.
  7. time zone Turn 14. The time zone anchors the collection time; 09:00 UTC is more precise than an unspecified nine o'clock.
  8. transformation log Turn 16. The transformation log records conversion of the sentinel and the calculation rule so the result can be traced to the original data.
  9. software environment Turn 18. The software environment identifies the tools and versions used to produce the result, supporting a repeatable computation.
  10. computational reproducibility Turn 20. Computational reproducibility checks whether the specified data and steps reproduce the result, not whether every scientific assumption is thereby validated.
Read the complete conversation

Dialogue in the book, page 64 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Variable names need units and missing-value definitions

Fictional data handoff using the lesson case: a shared dataset has variable names but no units, missing-value descriptions, or collection dates.

  1. Research analyst: Could you provide the units, missing-value definitions, and collection dates for the shared dataset?
  2. Data curator: Those details aren't included in the current metadata. I'll check the original collection records.
  3. Research analyst: Please identify any information that cannot be recovered rather than infer it from plausible values.
  4. Data curator: Understood. A column called duration doesn't establish whether its values represent seconds, minutes, or another unit.
  5. Research analyst: Exactly. We need that context for reproducibility, meaning another researcher can understand and repeat the analysis appropriately.
  6. Data curator: I'll also document provenance, including which collection records and transformations produced this version.
  7. Research analyst: Can you bring the available documentation to our next analysis meeting and keep unresolved definitions explicit?
  8. Data curator: Yes. I'll link the metadata to the repository version rather than overwrite the description without a history.
  9. Research analyst: I'll hold interpretations that depend on unknown units or missing-value codes until they're clarified.
  10. Data curator: Thank you. The handoff will distinguish verified definitions from gaps instead of giving the data false precision.
Zero may mean a measurement or a missing code

Fictional dataset review: a variable contains zeros, but the data dictionary does not explain whether zero is a legitimate observation or missing-value code.

  1. Data analyst: Could you clarify whether zero is a measured value or a missing-data code in this variable?
  2. Research data manager: The dictionary doesn't specify it. I need to check the collection form and import documentation.
  3. Data analyst: Please do that before I calculate the mean, because treating missing codes as observations could change it.
  4. Research data manager: Would replacing every zero with missing be the safest temporary interpretation while we wait?
  5. Data analyst: Not necessarily. That could remove valid observations. We should preserve the raw values and flag the uncertainty.
  6. Research data manager: I'll request the coding definition from the collection owner and retain the source record for provenance.
  7. Data analyst: Please include the relevant dataset version so the answer doesn't accidentally apply to another release.
  8. Research data manager: Agreed. I'll update metadata only with verified information and identify any unresolved historical change.
  9. Data analyst: I'll defer that calculation and explain exactly which definition is blocking interpretation.
  10. Research data manager: Then the next researcher can trace the decision instead of inheriting an undocumented assumption.
A repository file changed after analysis

Fictional reproducibility review: a shared data file was replaced after analysis; the analyst recorded a version identifier, but the current filename is unchanged.

  1. Research coordinator: The repository filename is unchanged. Can we assume the current file is the one used for analysis?
  2. Data analyst: No. The file was replaced afterward, and I recorded a version identifier for the analyzed dataset.
  3. Research coordinator: Please provide that identifier and the corresponding code version so the review can locate the actual inputs.
  4. Data analyst: I'll provide them and request the earlier data version from the repository manager.
  5. Research coordinator: Does the replacement establish that our published result is wrong, or only that reproducibility needs checking?
  6. Data analyst: It establishes a version difference, not a wrong result. We need the actual inputs and change history.
  7. Research coordinator: Please preserve the provenance record and identify whether the older version remains accessible.
  8. Data analyst: I will. If it isn't available, I'll report that gap rather than claim a verified rerun.
  9. Research coordinator: Bring the records to our review before anyone substitutes the latest file silently.
  10. Data analyst: Agreed. The handoff will identify what was analyzed, what changed, and what can currently be reproduced.

The missing-value code is not a temperature

Do not turn missing into zero

A confirmed Celsius column contains 10, 12, -999, and 14. The collector defines -999 as missing. The requested mean uses only observed temperatures, and the original file must be preserved.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Analyst: "There are ___ valid observations."

Answer and explanation

A. three

Ten, twelve, and fourteen are valid; the sentinel marks the missing reading.

2. Steward: "Their mean is ___ degrees Celsius."

Answer and explanation

D. 12

The three valid values sum to thirty-six, which divided by three equals twelve.

3. Analyst: "The raw sentinel is retained in the ___."

Answer and explanation

D. original file

The source record remains unchanged while a derived copy handles missing values.

4. Steward: "No missing temperature has been ___."

Answer and explanation

C. imputed

The calculation uses observed values rather than estimating a replacement temperature.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

REFERENCE
Course: Higher Education and Research English
Professional audience: faculty, postdoctoral researchers, graduate students, lab managers, research administrators, grant staff, ethics-board coordinators, and academic program leaders
Scope: All cases and figures are fictional. This book teaches research communication, not statistical, clinical, or legal advice or ethics approval. Follow the relevant institution, funder, jurisdiction, and journal requirements; obtain required review before conducting or changing research.
Lesson 07: Data Management and Reproducibility
Original fictional case: Research data steward Elena helps analyst Ravi prepare a small environmental dataset. The column t contains 20, 22, -999, 24, and 26. The collector confirms that t is air temperature in degrees Celsius and -999 means a failed reading, not a temperature or zero. The date label 04/05/26 is confirmed as May 4, 2026; readings were taken at 09:00 UTC. The raw file must remain unchanged. An analysis copy will mark the failed reading as missing and calculate the mean of the four valid values. The release needs a data dictionary, processing record, and identified software version.
Language workshop: The missing-value code is not a temperature
Communication goal: Make a dataset interpretable through verified units, missing-value rules, dates, and traceable processing.
Language guidance: The valid values are 20, 22, 24, and 26: their mean is 23 degrees Celsius. Treating -999 as a temperature would produce -181.4. Replacing it with zero would produce 18.4. Neither represents the stated valid-observation calculation.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): "What does the variable t represent?" / "The collector confirms degrees Celsius." / "Minus 999 marks a failed reading." / "Missing is not the same as zero." / "The raw file remains unchanged." / "The analysis copy marks that code as missing." / "The mean uses four valid observations." / "The valid-observation mean is 23 degrees Celsius." / "We have not imputed the failed reading." / "The collector confirms May 4, 2026." / "Record the date as 2026-05-04." / "The collection time is 09:00 UTC." / "The data dictionary must include units and missing codes." / "Log the conversion and the calculation rule." / "State the software version used for the analysis." / "Another analyst should be able to reproduce the reported mean."
Vocabulary: data dictionary: Definitions and coding rules for dataset variables. / metadata: Information describing data, collection, or processing. / variable name: The label assigned to a data field. / unit of measurement: The scale used to express a quantity. / missing-value code: A designated value representing absent information. / sentinel value: A special value used to mark a status rather than an observation. / failed reading: A measurement attempt without a valid recorded result. / valid observation: A recorded value meeting the applicable inclusion rules. / raw file: The original dataset before later cleaning or transformation. / analysis copy: A derived dataset used for analysis. / transformation log: A record of changes from source to derived data. / data cleaning: Applying documented rules to correct or prepare data. / imputation: Replacing missing values using a specified estimation method. / complete-case calculation: A calculation using records with required observed values. / date format: The convention used to represent a calendar date. / time zone: The reference used to interpret recorded times. / collection timestamp: The recorded date and time of observation. / version identifier: A label identifying a particular dataset or software revision. / software environment: The tools and versions used to run an analysis. / dependency: A required library or component for a process. / readme: An introductory file explaining a dataset or project. / repository: A managed location for preserving and accessing materials. / computational reproducibility: Reobtaining results with the specified data and computation. / data validation: Checks that data meet specified rules or expectations.
Speaking task: Read turns 1-8, saying minus nine hundred ninety-nine as a code and twenty-three degrees Celsius as the mean. Repeat turns 11-14 with May fourth and UTC clearly audible. Switch roles for the transfer; distinguish three observations from the mean of twelve.
Partner: Elena: Research data steward / Ravi: Analyst
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: A confirmed Celsius column contains 10, 12, -999, and 14. The collector defines -999 as missing. The requested mean uses only observed temperatures, and the original file must be preserved.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Ravi: I will add the software version and dependencies to the readme. Could you check that the instructions start from the raw file?
Elena: Document the software environment with the data and instructions. Even a simple calculation benefits from a traceable version, especially when this small example becomes part of a larger processing workflow.
Ravi: I will ask another analyst to follow the release instructions and reproduce the twenty-three-degree mean from the preserved source and documented rules.
Elena: That checks computational reproducibility. A matching result is useful, but it does not prove scientific validity. Keep the variable definitions with the calculation so readers can check both.
END REFERENCE

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.
